\mathbb{C}=\underline{\Psi}_{0}m_{a}m_{b}+\textsf{c.c.}
, from which it finally follows that the Weyl tensor of the conformally completed spacetime vanishes at null infinity. All in all, we can state the following result.

\boxed{C_{abcd}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}\begin{cases}0&\text{for }\Lambda\neq 0\\
0&\text{for }\Lambda=0\text{ if the topology of $\mathscr{I}^{+}$ is $\mathbb{S}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}$}\end{cases}}

(3.41)

This result is in contrast to Maxwell’s theory where we have
F_{ab}\,\,\cancel{\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}}\,\,0
. This leads us to introduce the
asymptotic Weyl tensor
defined as

K_{abcd}:=\Omega^{-1}C_{abcd}.

(3.42)

Recall from Side Note
3.B.2
that if a smooth tensor field vanishes at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
, then
\Omega^{-1}
times that tensor has a smooth limit at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. Thus, the asymptotic Weyl tensor is well-defined. Moreover, the Newman-Penrose scalars with an underline we introduced above, are mere auxiliary quantities. Their purpose was to simplify our proof of
C_{abcd}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0
. The Newman-Penrose scalars we shall use in the remainder of these notes are defined with respect to the asymptotic Weyl tensor.

\displaystyle{\Psi}_{4}

\displaystyle:=K_{abcd}\,n^{a}\bar{m}^{b}n^{c}m^{d}

\displaystyle{\Psi}_{3}

\displaystyle:=K_{abcd}\,\ell^{a}n^{b}\bar{m}^{c}n^{d}

\displaystyle{\Psi}_{2}

\displaystyle:=K_{abcd}\,\ell^{a}m^{b}\bar{m}^{c}n^{d}

\displaystyle{\Psi}_{1}

\displaystyle:=K_{abcd}\,\ell^{a}n^{b}\ell^{c}m^{d}

\displaystyle{\Psi}_{0}

\displaystyle:=K_{abcd}\,\ell^{a}m^{b}\ell^{c}m^{d}

(3.43)

These are the Newman-Penrose scalars we will use in the remainder of these notes. In particular, in the next chapter we will see that the smoothness of
K_{abcd}
together with the rescaling properties of the Newman-Penrose null tetrad imply the Peeling Properties for GR.

3.E
Exercises

Exercise 3.1

Use the field equations
\hat{R}_{ab}-\frac{1}{2}\hat{R}\,\hat{g}_{ab}+\Lambda\,g_{ab}=8\pi T_{ab}
with
\Lambda
to show that
\mathscr{I}^{+}
is timelike when
\Lambda<0
and timelike when
\Lambda>0
.

Exercise 3.2

Let
(\mathcal{N},q_{ab})
be a co-dimension one hypersurface which is embedded into the Lorentzian manifold
(\mathcal{M},g_{ab})
, where
q_{ab}:=\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{g}_{ab}
. Let
n^{a}
be the normal vector to
\mathcal{N}
and assume that
g_{ab}n^{a}n^{b}
. Show that the normal vector
n^{a}
is an eigenvector of the intrinsic metric
q_{ab}
with eigenvalue zero. That is, show that

q_{ab}n^{b}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0,

where in this context ‘
\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}
’ means “equality on
\mathcal{N}
”. Furthermore, show that
q_{ab}
has signature
(0,+,\cdots,+)
and is thus degenerate.

Exercise 3.3

Complete the construction of the Newman-Penrose null tetrad in
3.C
by showing that
\ell^{a}
is transverse to
all

u=
const. cross-section, while
m^{a}
is tangent to
all
these cross-sections.

Exercise 3.4

Prove the following claims:

a)

The constraint
C_{abcd}n^{d}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0
implies
\underline{\Psi}_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}\underline{\Psi}_{2}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}\underline{\Psi}_{3}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}\underline{\Psi}_{4}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0
.

b)

The real, symmetric, trace-free, and transverse tensor
\mathbb{C}_{ab}:=\epsilon_{mna}\epsilon_{pqb}C^{mnpq}
is equal to
\Psi_{0}m_{a}m_{b}+\bar{\Psi}_{0}\bar{m}_{a}\bar{m}_{b}
. Thus,
\mathbb{C}_{ab}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0\Leftrightarrow\Psi_{0}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0
.

\prescript{2}{}{\hat{\varepsilon}}:=i\,\hat{m}\wedge\hat{\bar{m}}
with

is the area element of a sphere of radius

\mathcal{C}

Exercise 3.6

Let

be a hypersurface which is either null or spacelike and let

. The electric and magnetic

-forms are then defined as

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle:=E_{I}\,\mathrm{d}y^{I}=\left.F_{ab}n^{b}\,\mathrm{d}x^{a}\right|_{\Sigma}

and

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle:=B_{I}\,\mathrm{d}y^{I}=\left.\prescript{\star}{}{F_{ab}}n^{b}\,\mathrm{d}x^{a}\right|_{\Sigma},

where
\left.T\right|_{\Sigma}
means “restriction of
T
to

” and

with

are coordinates on

. Prove the following claims:

If

is timelike), then the components

and

, (i.e., the electric and magnetic fields carry the same information as

).

hypersurface.

If

and

do

and

and

\frac{1}{2}\left(\left<E,E\right>+\left<B,B\right>\right)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}\left(\|\vec{E}\|^{2}+\|\vec{B}\|^{2}\right)&\text{for $\Sigma$ spacelike}\\
2|\Phi_{2}|^{2}&\text{for $\Sigma$ null},\end{cases}

where the bilinear inner product is defined as

for any

Chapter 4: Peeling and Universal Structure of Null Infinity

4.A
The Peeling Theorem for GR

In Chapter
3
, we have seen that the definition of asymptotic flatness in conjunction with the validity of Einstein’s field equations implies that the Weyl tensor satisfies
C_{abcd}n^{d}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0
. Hence, from purely local considerations on
\mathscr{I}^{+}
, we find that
\underline{\Psi}_{1}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}\underline{\Psi}_{2}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}\underline{\Psi}_{3}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}\underline{\Psi}_{4}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0
. In other words, eight of the ten components of the Weyl tensor vanish at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. The only component which is not immediately set to zero by the constraint
C_{abcd}n^{d}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0
is
\underline{\Psi}_{0}
.

This component only vanishes if we make an additional assumption on the topology of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. Namely, we need to assume that
\mathscr{I}^{+}\simeq\mathbb{S}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}
, which is physically well-motivated. We have argued that the symmetric, traceless, and transverse tensor
\mathbb{C}_{ab}
in conjunction with the Bianchi identities of the Weyl tensor then imply
\underline{\Psi}_{0}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0
. Hence, it follows that the Weyl tensor of the conformally completed spacetime
(\mathcal{M},g_{ab})
vanishes on
\mathscr{I}^{+}
,
C_{abcd}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0
.

This is an important fact, because it allows us to introduce a new tensor,
K_{abcd}:=\Omega^{-1}C_{abcd}
, which is smooth at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. We call it the
asymptotic Weyl tensor
. Since the asymptotic Weyl tensor is in general
not
zero at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
, we can use it to extract physics from it. In fact, this allows us to derive the Peeling Theorem for GR, which we do now.

To that end, we define, in the conformally completed spacetime
(\mathcal{M},g_{ab})
, the Newman-Penrose scalar

\Psi_{4}:=K_{abcd}n^{a}\bar{m}^{b}n^{c}\bar{m}^{d}.

(4.1)

A word on notation: Newman-Penrose scalars with an underline, such as
\underline{\Psi}_{i}
, are defined with respect to the Weyl tensor
C_{abcd}
. Scalars without an underline, such as
\Psi_{i}
, are defined with respect to the asymptotic Weyl tensor
K_{abcd}
. With this distinction out of the way, we note that we can write
\Psi_{4}
in (
4.1
) equivalently as

\Psi_{4}=\Omega^{-1}C_{abc}{}^{d}n^{a}\bar{m}^{b}n^{c}\bar{m}_{d}.

(4.2)

We have just substituted
K_{abcd}
with
\Omega^{-1}C_{abcd}
and we have raised the last index of the Weyl tensor. The reason for doing so is that the Weyl tensor
C_{abc}{}^{d}
, with the last index raised, is conformally invariant. Hence, we have
C_{abc}{}^{d}=\hat{C}_{abc}{}^{d}
, where
\hat{C}_{abc}{}^{d}
is the Weyl tensor with respect to the
physical
metric
\hat{g}_{ab}
. We therefore get

\displaystyle\Psi_{4}=\Omega^{-1}\hat{C}_{abc}{}^{d}n^{a}\bar{m}^{b}n^{c}\bar{m}_{d}.

(4.3)

To proceed, we recall that the physical Newman-Penrose tetrad is related to the one of the conformally completed spacetime via the relations

\displaystyle n^{a}

\displaystyle=\hat{n}^{a},

\displaystyle\ell^{a}

\displaystyle=\Omega^{-2}\,\hat{\ell}^{a},

\displaystyle m^{a}=\Omega^{-1}\,\hat{m}^{a}.

(4.4)

After using these rescaling relations, we find that the scalar
\Psi_{4}
can be written in terms of the
physical
fields as

\displaystyle\Psi_{4}

\displaystyle=\Omega^{-1}\hat{C}_{abc}{}^{d}\hat{n}^{a}\left(\Omega^{-1}\,\hat{\bar{m}}^{b}\right)\hat{n}^{c}\left(\Omega\,\hat{\bar{m}}_{d}\right)

\displaystyle=\Omega^{-1}\hat{C}_{abc}{}^{d}\hat{n}^{a}\hat{\bar{m}}^{b}\hat{n}^{c}\hat{\bar{m}}_{d},

(4.5)

where we used
\bar{m}_{a}=\bar{m}^{b}g_{ab}=\left(\Omega^{-1}\hat{\bar{m}}^{b}\right)\Omega^{2}\hat{g}_{ab}=\Omega\,\hat{\bar{m}}_{a}
. We therefore obtain a relation between the physical scalar
\hat{\Psi}_{4}
and the Weyl scalar with respect to the asymptotic Weyl tensor,
\Psi_{4}
:

\Psi_{4}(\Omega,u,\theta,\phi)=r\,\hat{\Psi}_{4}(\Omega,u,\theta,\phi).

(4.6)

This implies that the physical scalar
\hat{\Psi}_{4}
decays like

\hat{\Psi}_{4}(r,u,\theta,\phi)=\frac{\Psi^{\circ}_{4}(u,\theta,\phi)}{r}+\mathcal{O}(r^{-2}),

(4.7)

where we have introduced
\Psi^{\circ}_{4}(u,\theta,\phi):=\left.\Psi_{4}(\Omega,u,\theta,\phi)\right|_{\Omega=0}
, which is the zeroth order term in the Taylor expansion of
\Psi_{4}
around
\Omega=0
. This is the first peeling property and the other peeling properties follow in a similar manner (see Exercise
4.1
). All in all, we find

\boxed{\begin{aligned} \hat{\Psi}_{4}(r,u,\theta,\phi)&=\frac{\Psi^{\circ}_{4}(u,\theta,\phi)}{r}+\mathcal{O}(r^{-2})\\
\hat{\Psi}_{3}(r,u,\theta,\phi)&=\frac{\Psi_{3}^{\circ}(u,\theta,\phi)}{r^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(r^{-3})\\
\hat{\Psi}_{2}(r,u,\theta,\phi)&=\frac{\Psi_{2}^{\circ}(u,\theta,\phi)}{r^{3}}+\mathcal{O}(r^{-4})\\
\hat{\Psi}_{1}(r,u,\theta,\phi)&=\frac{\Psi_{1}^{\circ}(u,\theta,\phi)}{r^{4}}+\mathcal{O}(r^{-5})\\
\hat{\Psi}_{0}(r,u,\theta,\phi)&=\frac{\Psi_{0}^{\circ}(u,\theta,\phi)}{r^{5}}+\mathcal{O}(r^{-6})\end{aligned}}

(4.8)

The scalar
\Psi_{i}^{\circ}
always denotes the value of
\Psi_{i}
at
\mathscr{I}^{+}
, i.e., it always stands for the zeroth order of the Taylor expansion of
\Psi_{i}
(the scalar with respect to the asymptotic Weyl tensor) around
\Omega=0
.

With this, we have proven the Peeling Theorem for GR. The theorem tells us that the different components of the physical Weyl tensor
\hat{C}_{abcd}
decay, or ‘‘peel off’’, at different rates
8
8

8

It is again easy to remember at which rate
\hat{\Psi}_{i}\propto\frac{1}{r^{n}}
decays. The correct behavior follows from
i+n=5
.
as one approaches
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. This peeling leads again, just as in the case of electromagnetism, to a neat separation of different modes. In fact, the peeling property of
\hat{\Psi}_{4}
suggests that it encodes the radiation field, since it decays like
\frac{1}{r}
. Conversely, one can guess that
\hat{\Psi}_{2}
encodes the “coulombic” information of the gravitational field (i.e., the mass of the source which generates the field). This is motivated by the following simple example: In the Schwarzschild spacetime, we have
\hat{g}_{ab}=\hat{\eta}_{ab}+\mathcal{O}(r^{-1})
, which then implies
\hat{C}_{abcd}\propto\frac{1}{r^{3}}
. This is a first indication that
\hat{\Psi}_{2}\propto\frac{1}{r^{3}}
carries information about the mass of the source and the gravitational force. This is what we call the “coulombic” information of the gravitational field, using an obvious analogy with electromagnetism.

To actually extract physical information from the Weyl scalars
\hat{\Psi}_{i}
such as energy (mass), momentum, and angular momentum of the source, or whether or not there is gravitational radiation, requires more work and the introduction of new ideas. The reason is that unlike in special relativistic theories (think of Maxwell, Yang-Mills fields, the Klein-Gordon field, etc.) we do
not
have an energy momentum tensor for the gravitational field and, related to that issue, in GR we do not have a preferred symmetry group. Special relativistic theories are invariant under Poincaré transformations because they are defined on a fixed background manifold equipped with a fixed metric – the Minkowski metric. In GR, on the other hand, the manifold and the metric are determined from within the theory as a solution of Einstein’s equations. Moreover, the theory is generally covariant. Hence, no preferred coordinate systems and no preferred coordinate transformations between these systems exist with respect to which we could define notions such as energy and momentum.

What we will see in this and the next chapter, however, is that in asymptotically Minkowski spacetimes, there is a way of singling out preferred coordinates and preferred coordinate transformations, which will ultimately enable us to define conserved quantities.

Before going into more details, in the next subsection we will illustrate the Peeling Theorem for the Schwarzschild and the Kerr-Newman family of spacetimes.

4.B
Illustrating the Newman-Penrose Formalism using Black Hole Solutions

We have shown that the Newman-Penrose scalars of GR
\hat{\Psi}_{i}
obey the Peeling Theorem. Now we want to put the theory to a test and explicitly illustrate the Peeling Theorem! For technical reasons, we focus on simple and exact solutions, which means we will not be talking about spacetimes containing radiation. Rather, we will show that the spacetimes under consideration possess a vanishing
\Psi_{4}^{\circ}
, consistent with the fact that they are devoid of gravitational waves. Moreover, we check whether the “coulombic” part, i.e.,
\Psi^{\circ}_{2}
, has the correct dependencies on spacetime parameters and behaves in a manner we would expect. We motivate the reader to redo the computations of the examples we provide here.

Concretely, we consider the Schwarzschild solution as a warm-up exercise in
4.B.1
and then we move to the Kerr-Newman solution in subsection
4.B.2
, which describes a charged and rotating black hole. Since these solutions are stationary, we expect
\Psi^{\circ}_{4}=0
, while the “coulombic” part should be proportional to the mass,
\Psi_{2}^{\circ}\propto M
. Contrary to the electrodynamic examples we studied in
2.B
, we will not compute quantities such as the energy and momentum associated with the gravitational field. Introducing such notions for the gravitational field is a subtle issue and we will elaborate more on this in Chapters
6
and
7
.

4.B.1
The Schwarzschild Black Hole

Our aim is to compute the physical Newman-Penrose scalars
\hat{\Psi}_{i}
for the Schwarzschild solution of Einstein’s field equations. Thus, physically speaking, we consider a non-rotating, uncharged black hole of mass
M
. In the Schwarzschild chart
\{t,r,\theta,\phi\}
, the line element takes the well-known form

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\hat{s}^{2}=-\left(1-\frac{2M}{r}\right)\mathrm{d}t^{2}+\frac{1}{1-\frac{2M}{r}}\mathrm{d}r^{2}+r^{2}\mathrm{d}\theta^{2}+r^{2}\sin{\theta}\,\mathrm{d}\phi^{2}.

(4.9)

In order to be able to use the Newman-Penrose formalism, our first step has to be to rewrite the above line element in outgoing Eddington-Finkelstein coordinates
\{u,\Omega,\theta,\phi\}
, with
u:=t-r-2M\ln\left(\frac{r}{2M}-1\right)
, for
r>2M
, and
\Omega:=\frac{1}{r}
. In the new chart, the above line element reads

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\hat{s}^{2}=-\left(1-2M\Omega\right)\mathrm{d}u^{2}+2\Omega^{-2}\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}\Omega+\Omega^{-2}\mathrm{d}\theta^{2}+\Omega^{-2}\sin{\theta}\,\mathrm{d}\phi^{2}.

(4.10)

From this line element, we can read off the physical metric
\hat{g}_{ab}
in the chart
\{u,\Omega,\theta,\phi\}
and consequently calculate the Newman-Penrose null tetrad
\{\hat{n}^{a},\hat{\ell}^{a},\hat{m}^{a},\hat{\bar{m}}^{a}\}
, the Weyl tensor
\hat{C}_{abcd}(\hat{g})
and, finally, the corresponding
\hat{\Psi}_{i}
.

Let us begin with the null tetrad. In Chapter
3
, we have provided a recipe for constructing the null tetrad for a curved spacetime and we encourage the reader to follow the steps of that recipe. Here, we will simply sketch the procedure: Based on
\hat{n}^{a}=\nabla^{a}\Omega
in the coordinate chart
\{u,\Omega,\theta,\phi\}
we chose
\hat{n}^{a}\propto\delta_{1}{}^{a}
where the proportionality coefficient can be chosen for convenience. We go for
\hat{n}^{a}=\sqrt{2}\delta_{1}{}^{a}
. Then, via
\hat{n}^{a}\hat{\ell}_{a}=-1
we find the condition
\sqrt{2}\hat{\ell}^{0}/\Omega^{2}=-1
which determines
\hat{\ell}^{0}
. Further, from
\hat{\ell}^{a}\hat{\ell}_{a}=0
and with the knowledge that in this coordinate chart
\hat{\ell}^{a}
has no components in
\theta,\phi
-direction, we deduce
\hat{\ell}^{0}\hat{\ell}^{0}\left(-1+2M\Omega\right)+4\hat{\ell}^{0}\hat{\ell}^{1}/\Omega=0
. With the latter equation we find
\hat{\ell}^{1}
and, thus, have a full description of
\hat{\ell}^{a}
. In similar fashion, we obtain the vectors
\hat{m}^{a},\hat{\bar{m}}^{a}
. Overall, this results in

\displaystyle\hat{n}^{a}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\,\delta_{1}{}^{a},

\displaystyle\hat{\ell}^{a}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Omega^{2}\,\delta_{0}{}^{a}+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\Omega^{4}(-1+2M\Omega)\,\delta_{1}{}^{a},

\displaystyle\hat{m}^{a}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Omega\,\delta_{2}{}^{a}+\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}\sin\theta}\Omega\,\delta_{3}{}^{a}.

(4.11)

Next, we compute the physical Weyl tensor and we find the following non-zero components (we do not display components which can be obtained from the components listed below by symmetries of the Weyl tensor):

\displaystyle\hat{C}_{0101}

\displaystyle=-2M\Omega^{-1}

\displaystyle\hat{C}_{0202}

\displaystyle=-\left(2M\Omega-1\right)M\Omega

\displaystyle\hat{C}_{0212}

\displaystyle=-M\Omega^{-1}

\displaystyle\hat{C}_{0303}

\displaystyle=-(2M\Omega-1)M\Omega\,\sin^{2}{\theta}

\displaystyle\hat{C}_{0313}

\displaystyle=-M\Omega^{-1}\,\sin^{2}{\theta}

\displaystyle\hat{C}_{2323}

\displaystyle=2M\Omega^{-1}\,\sin^{2}{\theta}.

(4.12)

We now have all the ingredients needed to compute the Newman-Penrose scalars
\hat{\Psi}_{i}
and we find

\displaystyle\hat{\Psi}_{4}(u,\Omega,\theta,\phi)=\hat{C}_{abc}{}^{d}\hat{n}^{a}\hat{\bar{m}}^{b}\hat{n}^{c}\hat{m}_{d}

\displaystyle=\ 0

\displaystyle\hat{\Psi}_{3}(u,\Omega,\theta,\phi)=\hat{C}_{abc}{}^{d}\hat{\ell}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}\hat{\bar{m}}^{c}\hat{n}_{d}

\displaystyle=\ 0

\displaystyle\hat{\Psi}_{2}(u,\Omega,\theta,\phi)=\hat{C}_{abc}{}^{d}\hat{\ell}^{a}\hat{m}^{b}\hat{\bar{m}}^{c}\hat{n}_{d}

\displaystyle=\ -M\,\Omega^{3}

\displaystyle\hat{\Psi}_{1}(u,\Omega,\theta,\phi)=\hat{C}_{abc}{}^{d}\hat{\ell}^{a}\hat{n}^{b}\hat{\ell}^{c}\hat{m}_{d}

\displaystyle=\ 0

\displaystyle\hat{\Psi}_{0}(u,\Omega,\theta,\phi)=\hat{C}_{abc}{}^{d}\hat{\ell}^{a}\hat{m}^{b}\hat{\ell}^{c}\hat{m}_{d}

\displaystyle=\ 0,

(4.13)

from which we can simply read off the leading order contributions
\Psi^{\circ}_{i}
,

\displaystyle\Psi^{\circ}_{4}=\Psi^{\circ}_{3}=\Psi^{\circ}_{1}=\Psi^{\circ}_{0}

\displaystyle=0

and

\displaystyle\Psi^{\circ}_{2}=-M,

(4.14)

which immediately confirms the Peeling Theorem for the Schwarzschild spacetime. As anticipated,
\Psi^{\circ}_{2}
is not zero since it encodes the “coulombic” information, i.e., it carries information about the mass which is sourcing the field. Also, because
\Psi^{\circ}_{4}
vanishes, we find, reassuringly, that there is no gravitational radiation in this spacetime.

This was a rather simple example and intended to be more of a proof of concept for the Newman-Penrose formalism. In the next subsection, we consider the computationally more complicated case of the Kerr-Newman solution and explicitly confirm the validity of the Peeling Theorem also for this family of spacetimes.

4.B.2
The Kerr-Newman Family of Black Holes

Let us now turn our attention towards the Kerr-Newman black hole solution. That is, we consider a black hole of mass
M
, charge
Q
, and angular momentum
J
. In the metric, the angular momentum is encoded through
a:=J/M
and in outgoing Eddington-Finkelstein coordinates
\{u,r,\theta,\phi\}
, the Kerr-Newman line element reads

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\hat{s}^{2}=

\displaystyle-\left(1-\frac{2Mr-Q^{2}}{r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}u^{2}-2\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}r-\frac{2a\sin^{2}\theta}{r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta}(2Mr-Q^{2})\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}\phi+2a\sin^{2}\theta\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}\phi

\displaystyle+(r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta\left(r^{2}+a^{2}+\frac{a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}{r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta}(2Mr-Q^{2})\right)\mathrm{d}\phi^{2}.

(4.15)

We perform a conformal completion by introducing the new coordinate
\Omega:=\frac{1}{r}
and multiplying the physical line element by
\Omega^{2}
.
9
9

9

Strictly speaking, the conformal completion is not necessary. In order to compute the Newman-Penrose scalars
\hat{\Psi}_{i}
and verify their Peeling properties, we do not need to work in the conformally completed spacetime. However, we do need to know in which “direction” to go in order to find
\mathscr{I}^{+}
and check that the
\hat{\Psi}_{i}
fall off as predicted by the Peeling Theorem. For this purpose, it is useful to work in the
\{u,\Omega,\theta,\phi\}
chart.
In the chart
\{u,\Omega,\theta,\phi\}
, the physical line element becomes

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\hat{s}^{2}=

\displaystyle-\left(1-\frac{2M\Omega^{-1}-Q^{2}}{\Omega^{-2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta}\right)\mathrm{d}u^{2}+2\Omega^{-2}\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}\Omega-\frac{2a\sin^{2}\theta}{\Omega^{-2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta}(2M\Omega-Q^{2})\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}\phi

\displaystyle+2a\sin^{2}\theta\,\mathrm{d}\Omega\,\mathrm{d}\phi+(\Omega^{-2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\theta^{2}

\displaystyle+\sin^{2}\theta\left(\Omega^{-2}+a^{2}+\frac{a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}{\Omega^{-2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta}(2M\Omega^{-1}-Q^{2})\right)\mathrm{d}\phi^{2}.

(4.16)

It can easily be confirmed that the above line element reduces to the Schwarzschild expression if one takes the limits
Q\to 0
and
a\to 0
. As one can imagine, based on this rather lengthy expression for the line element, the construction of the Newman-Penrose tetrads is more involved than in the Schwarzschild case. The recipe is the same, though, and we encourage the reader to verify that in the end one finds, in the chart
\{u,\Omega,\theta,\phi\}
, the following null tetrad:

\displaystyle\hat{n}^{a}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\,\delta_{1}{}^{a}

\displaystyle\hat{\ell}^{a}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Omega^{2}}{\sqrt{2}\left(\Omega^{-2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta\right)}\left((\Omega^{-2}+a^{2})\,\delta_{0}{}^{a}\,+\,\Omega^{2}\frac{\Omega^{-2}+a^{2}+Q^{2}-2M\Omega^{-1}}{2}\delta_{1}{}^{a}+a\,\delta_{3}{}^{a}\right)

\displaystyle\hat{m}^{a}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}(\Omega^{-1}+i\,a\,\cos\theta)}\left(i\,a\,\sin\theta\delta\,_{0}{}^{a}+\delta_{2}{}^{a}+\frac{i}{\sin\theta}\delta_{3}{}^{a}\right).

(4.17)

As a consistency check, observe that for
Q\to 0
and
a\to 0
we recover the null tetrad of the Schwarzschild spacetime. Moreover, notice that other choices for the null tetrad are possible which nevertheless satisfy the normalization and cross-normalization properties.

At this point, we need to compute the Weyl tensor. Because of the length and complexity of the resulting expressions, we do not display the individual components here. Rather, we move directly to computing the Newman-Penrose scalars, for which we obtain

\displaystyle\hat{\Psi}_{4}(u,\Omega,\theta,\phi)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\hat{\Psi}_{3}(u,\Omega,\theta,\phi)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\hat{\Psi}_{2}(u,\Omega,\theta,\phi)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Omega^{3}\left(M-\left(Q^{2}+i\,Ma\cos\theta\right)\Omega\right)}{\left(a\cos\theta\,\Omega-i\right)^{3}\left(a\cos\theta\,\Omega+i\right)}

\displaystyle\hat{\Psi}_{1}(u,\Omega,\theta,\phi)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\hat{\Psi}_{0}(u,\Omega,\theta,\phi)

\displaystyle=0.

(4.18)

As expected, we find that
\hat{\Psi}_{4}
vanishes, while the “coulombic” part of the Weyl tensor is non-trivial. We can expand
\hat{\Psi}_{2}
in powers of
\Omega
and we find that the lowest order term is given by

\displaystyle\hat{\Psi}_{2}(u,\Omega,\theta,\phi)

\displaystyle=-M\,\Omega^{3}+\mathcal{O}(\Omega^{4})

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle\Psi^{\circ}_{2}(u,\theta,\phi)

\displaystyle=-M.

(4.19)

This is precisely the same result as for the Schwarzschild spacetime and, moreover, we have confirmed that the Peeling properties are satisfied.

This is not the end of the story, though. As the Kerr-Newman black hole is charged, it also generates an electromagnetic field and we should be able to determine, via the Newman-Penrose formalism, whether it emits electromagnetic radiation. Of course, we expect that the Kerr-Newman black hole simply represents a Coulomb charge in a curved background, but we would like to confirm that via the formalism.

First of all, we need the Maxwell
2
-form for the Kerr-Newman spacetime. In the chart
\{u,\Omega,\theta,\phi\}
, it is given by (see for instance
[
6
]
)

\displaystyle F_{01}

\displaystyle=-F_{10}=-\frac{Q\left(1-a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta\,\Omega^{2}\right)}{\left(1+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta\,\Omega^{2}\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle F_{02}

\displaystyle=-F_{20}=-\frac{2a^{2}Q\,\cos\theta\,\sin\theta\,\Omega^{3}}{\left(1+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta\,\Omega^{2}\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle F_{13}

\displaystyle=-F_{31}=-\frac{a\,Q\,\sin^{2}\theta\left(1-a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta\,\Omega^{2}\right)}{\left(1+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta\,\Omega^{2}\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle F_{23}

\displaystyle=-F_{32}=-\frac{2a\,Q\,\cos\theta\,\sin\theta\left(1+a^{2}\Omega^{2}\right)\Omega}{\left(1+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta\,\Omega^{2}\right)^{2}}.

(4.20)

To convince oneself that this is the correct expression for the Maxwell
2
-form, one can use symbolic manipulation software, such as
Mathematica
, to verify that (a) the trace of
T_{ab}:=g^{cd}F_{ac}F_{bd}-\frac{1}{4}g_{ab}F_{cd}F_{ef}g^{ce}g^{df}
vanishes, as it should, and that (b) the equation
G_{ab}=\kappa\,T_{ab}
is satisfied.

Using the null tetrad (
4.B.2
), we can calculate the physical Newman-Penrose scalars
\hat{\Phi}_{i}
for the electromagnetic field. We obtain

\displaystyle\hat{\Phi}_{2}(u,\Omega,\theta,\phi)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\hat{\Phi}_{1}(u,\Omega,\theta,\phi)

\displaystyle=\frac{Q\,\Omega^{2}}{2(i-a\cos\theta\,\Omega)^{2}}

\displaystyle\hat{\Phi}_{0}(u,\Omega,\theta,\phi)

\displaystyle=0.

(4.21)

Since
\hat{\Phi}_{2}=0
, we can confirm that there is no electromagnetic radiation. By expanding
\hat{\Phi}_{1}
in
\Omega
around
\Omega=0
, we find

\displaystyle\hat{\Phi}_{1}(u,\Omega,\theta,\phi)

\displaystyle=-\frac{Q}{2}\Omega^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\Omega^{3})

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle\Phi^{\circ}_{1}(u,\theta,\phi)

\displaystyle=-\frac{Q}{2}.

(4.22)

This is in perfect agreement with the Peeling Theorem and, moreover, we find exactly the same result we found in
2.B
for the Coulomb charge. This further confirms our intuition that the Kerr-Newman black hole describes a Coulomb charge in a curved background. Indeed, we find for the total charge in the spacetime, unsurprisingly,

\displaystyle\text{Total charge}=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\oint_{\mathbb{S}^{2}\text{ on }\mathscr{I}^{+}}\operatorname{\textsf{Re}}\left[\Phi^{\circ}_{1}\right]\sin\theta\,\mathrm{d}\theta\,\mathrm{d}\phi=Q.

(4.23)

To summarize, in this example we have illustrated that the Kerr-Newman family of spacetimes

a)

obey the Peeling properties of the gravitational field;

b)

also obey the Peeling properties of the electromagnetic field;

c)

has a non-vanishing “coulombic” part of the gravitational Newman-Penrose scalars which encodes the mass of the black hole and it has a non-vanishing coulombic part of the electromagnetic Newman-Penrose scalars which encode the charge of the black hole.

4.C
Extracting Physics

So far we have seen that, with a little more effort than for electromagnetism, the Peeling Theorem also holds for GR. This theorem tells us at what rates the different Weyl scalars decay as one approaches
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. This theorem also confirms our intuition, that the gravitational field becomes asymptotically flat and only differs by terms of order
\mathcal{O}(r^{-1})
from the Minkowski metric.

Now we would like to go a step further and extract some actual physics. For instance, we know that the coalescence of compact objects is caused by a loss of energy due to gravitational waves. We also know that when two compact objects merge, the sudden emission of gravitational waves can cause a “kick”. In principle, such a “kick” could eject the remnant of the merger from the galaxy. From observations we can also learn that the rest mass of the remnant is less than the rest masses of the bodies which coalesced. Supposedly, the difference in mass was converted into energy and radiated away by gravitational waves.

In all these examples we made implicit or explicit use of some notion of energy and momentum. Not just of material bodies, but of the gravitational field itself. Or, if not about the gravitational field in general, then about gravitational waves which have traveled far from their source. Can we make these implicit notions mathematically precise?

To answer the question, we will again take inspiration from Maxwell’s theory. In that case, spacetime symmetries play an important role for the definition of energy and momentum of the field. More precisely, we make use of the Poincaré group, which is generated by time translation, spatial translations, rotations, and boosts. What we call energy-momentum and angular momentum of the field are then quantities associated with the invariance of the action functional under spacetime translations and rotations. Alternatively, we can also defined energy-momentum and angular momentum as the generators of spacetime translations and rotations.

The reason we can make use of the Poincaré group is Noether’s theorem and the fixed background metric. Having a fixed metric provides us with a universal background structure which is present in all special relativistic theories (think of Klein-Gordon fields, Dirac fields, Yang-Mills fields, etc.). Ultimately, it is the invariance of the background structure under certain coordinate transformations which determines the symmetry (or isometry) group. To be more precise, the Poincaré group is formed by generators
\mathfrak{p}^{a}
, which satisfy
\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{p}}\eta_{ab}=0
. The latter is the mathematical statement that the background structure (here, the Minkowski metric) is invariant under transformations generated by
\mathfrak{p}^{a}
.

Our strategy for GR is to emulate what we learned from electrodynamics: We start by looking for a universal structure which is common to all asymptotically Minkowski spacetimes – this is the class of spacetimes we are interested in. That is, we look for a geometric structure which is common to all these spacetimes and then we ask which generators of “infinitesimal” coordinate transformations leave this structure invariant.

Of course, in GR we do not have a preferred subgroup of the diffeomorphism group in general. However, we are not interested in generic spacetimes. Rather, we only consider the sector of GR which consists of asymptotically Minkowski spacetimes. For this sector, we can reasonably expect the existence of a universal structure. The intuition is quite simple: At
\mathscr{I}^{+}
, the spacetime metric tends to the Minkowski metric, which is the universal background structure we seek. However, we will see that because the asymptotic Minkowski metric can be obtained in many different ways, the isometry group will
not
be the Poincaré group, as one may intuitively expect, but a much larger group which is known as the Bondi-Metzner-Sachs (BMS) group.

Our task in the remainder of this chapter is to determine the universal structure. The BMS group will then be the subject of the next chapter.

4.D
Universal Structure of Asymptotically Minkowski Spacetimes

What we seek is a structure which is common to all spacetimes under consideration. Since we only consider the sector of asymptotically Minkowski spacetimes, we can take the following properties for granted:

1.

The spacetime admits a boundary, which is
\mathscr{I}
. This boundary has the topology
\mathbb{S}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}
.

2.

The boundary is a null
3
-manifold described by
(q_{ab},n^{a})
, where
n^{a}
is the null normal to
\mathscr{I}
,
q_{ab}
is the degenerate metric of signature
(0,+,+)
, and
n^{a}
is also the null direction on
\mathscr{I}
, i.e.,
q_{ab}n^{b}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0
.

3.

We can work in a divergence-free conformal frame. This means we can make use of the property
\nabla_{a}n_{b}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0
, which in turn implies
\mathcal{L}_{n}g_{ab}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0
. Pulling back the latter equation to
\mathscr{I}
results in
\mathcal{L}_{n}q_{ab}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0
. This is a property which is intrinsic to
\mathscr{I}
.

What these properties tell us is the following: First of all, we can think of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
as being a cylinder (cf. Figure
9(a)
), which is ‘‘ruled’’ by null geodesics of the form
10
10

10

This means there is one line, parametrized by
u\in\mathbb{R}
, for each value of
(\theta_{0},\phi_{0})\in[0,\pi]\times[0,2\pi)
.

(u,\theta_{0},\phi_{0})
. These null geodesics are the integral curves of the null normal
n^{a}
(see again Figure
9(a)
).

(a)

Null infinity as a cylinder

(b)

The space of generators

Figure

9
:
Panel (a) shows a visualization of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
as a cylinder. Null infinity is ruled by the integral lines of
n^{a}
, which are of the form
(u,\theta_{0},\phi_{0})
. Each cross-section
\mathcal{C}
of constant
u
has the topology of
\mathbb{S}^{2}
and is equipped with the same metric
s_{ab}
. Panel (b) shows the projection from
\mathscr{I}^{+}
to the space of generators
\mathbb{G}
. This space has the topology
\mathbb{S}^{2}
and each integral line of
n^{a}
is projected to a point in
\mathbb{G}
. There is a unique, non-degenerate metric
\underline{q}_{ab}
on
\mathbb{G}
, which carries the same information as the degenerate metric
q_{ab}
and whose pullback to
\mathscr{I}^{+}
generates
q_{ab}
.

On this cylinder, we have an intrinsic (degenerate) metric
q_{ab}
which is preserved under the Lie drag along
n^{a}
;
\mathcal{L}_{n}q_{ab}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0
. This means that the intrinsic metric does not change in the
n^{a}
direction (i.e., along the
u
coordinate) and therefore
q_{ab}
can only depend on
\theta
and
\phi
.

Furthermore, every cross-sections
\mathcal{C}
of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
, determined by
u=
const., has the topology
\mathbb{S}^{2}
. Each cross-section is equipped with a metric
s_{ab}
, which is obtained by pulling back
q_{ab}
from
\mathscr{I}^{+}
to
\mathcal{C}
,

s_{ab}:=\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{q_{ab}}.

(4.24)

Because
q_{ab}
is degenerate, there is at least one null vector.
11
11

11

A null vector in the sense of linear algebra: A vector
v\neq 0
which satisfies
Mv=0
, where
M
is a square matrix. In other words, an eigenvector with eigenvalue zero.
This null vector is
n^{a}
because we know from point 2 that
q_{ab}n^{b}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0
. Moreover, the metric
q_{ab}
does not change as we move in the vertical direction of the cylinder shown in Figure
9
, i.e., when we move along
n^{a}
. That is because
\mathcal{L}_{n}q_{ab}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0
. From this we learn that
s_{ab}
looks exactly the same on each cross-section
\mathcal{C}
and that all metric information is actually contained in
s_{ab}
. It also follows thatthe metric
q_{ab}
is necessarily of the form

q_{ab}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&s_{22}&s_{23}\\
0&s_{23}&s_{33}\end{pmatrix}

(4.25)

with respect to the basis
\{n^{a},m^{a},\bar{m}^{a}\}
. Also,
s_{ab}
is only a function of the coordinates in the plane perpendicular to
n^{a}
. Since
q_{ab}
does not change in the direction
n^{a}
(it only changes in the two directions perpendicular to
n^{a}
), we can imagine that on
\mathscr{I}^{+}
, we are stacking one metric
s_{ab}
on top of another, since on each cross-section
\mathcal{C}
we have the same metric. This is a highly redundant description of the intrinsic geometry of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
!

Therefore, it makes sense to introduce the
projector

\pi:\mathscr{I}^{+}\to\mathbb{G}
, as illustrated in Figure
9(b)
. The space
\mathbb{G}
is called the
base space
or the
space of orbits
or
the space of generators
– the terminology in the literature varies, but it all means the same. Namely, the projector maps every generator (i.e., every vertical line of the cylinder) of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
to a single point in
\mathbb{G}
(see Figure
9(b)
). Since every generator is mapped to a
different
point in
\mathbb{G}
(meaning that
\pi
is
injective
), we can think of every point in
\mathbb{G}
as representing precisely one generator. Therefore, in these notes, we refer to it as the
space of generators
. Let us also note that because
\mathscr{I}^{+}
has the topology
\mathbb{S}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}
, the space of generators necessarily has the topology of
\mathbb{S}^{2}
, since we project along the
\mathbb{R}
direction. Moreover, the projector
\pi
allows us to introduce a metric on
\mathbb{G}
, which is free of all the redundancies which plague
q_{ab}
. In fact, the metric
\underline{q}_{ab}
on
\mathbb{G}
is implicitly defined via a pullback induced by
\pi
:

\pi^{*}\underline{q}_{ab}:=q_{ab}.

(4.26)

We summarize the situation in simple words: The projector “collapses” the infinite tower of stacked-up cross-sections
\mathcal{C}
, which are all equipped with the same metric, into a single space. This space has the topology
\mathbb{S}^{2}
and it is equipped with a unique, non-degenerate metric,
\underline{q}_{ab}
. In a sense, the projector isolates all the information contained in
q_{ab}
, thus providing us with the simplest possible description of that information.

What we have described in this subsection so far is the universal structure of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. To be more precise, we
(q_{ab},n^{a})

in a given divergence-free conformal frame
the
universal structure
of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. However, recall from Chapter
3
that in a conformal frame we have the freedom of perform a rescaling transformation of the form
\Omega\mapsto\Omega^{\prime}=\omega\,\Omega
, where
\omega
is a smooth, nowhere zero function with
\mathcal{L}_{n}\omega\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0
. Under this rescaling, the intrinsic metric and the null normal transform as

\boxed{\begin{aligned} q^{\prime}_{ab}&=\omega^{2}\,q_{ab}&\text{and}&&n^{\prime a}&=\omega^{-1}n^{a}\end{aligned}}

(4.27)

This transformation does not affect any aspect of the universal structure we have discussed. Thus, the pair
(q^{\prime}_{ab},n^{\prime a})
is an equally admissible description of the universal structure, provided that
(q_{ab},n^{a})\mapsto(q^{\prime}_{ab}=\omega^{2}\,q_{ab},n^{\prime a}=\omega^{-1}\,n^{a})
. In other words, the universal structure is described by an equivalence class of pairs
(q_{ab},n^{a})
under the conformal rescaling operation discussed here.

In the next chapter, we will see that this universal structure allows us to introduce an asymptotic symmetry group for the class of asymptotically Minkowski spacetimes.

.

Chapter 5: The Bondi-Metzner-Sachs Group

The universal structure of asymptotically Minkowskian spacetimes provides us with a compact mathematical description of the most fundamental attributes of this class of spacetimes. A natural first step is to find all transformations which leave this universal structure invariant. In other words, we are seeking the asymptotic isometry group of spacetime metrics which are asymptotically Minkowskian. Naïvely, we would expect to recover the Poincaré group, i.e., the isometry group of Minkowski space. However, we will see that this is not the case. The class of asymptotically Minkowski spacetimes admits a
larger
group which contains the Poincaré group as a subgroup. This enlargement is actually what allows us to account for gravitational radiation. As we will see later on, the asymptotic symmetry group plays a crucial role in the study of conserved charges on
\mathscr{I}^{+}
and are therefore of direct relevance for extracting physics.

Qualitatively, a symmetry group consists of diffeomorphisms which leave the structure to be studied invariant. In our case, the structure to be preserved is the universal structure of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
, which is characterized, as we recall, by the following properties:

1)

\mathscr{I}^{+}
has the topology
\mathbb{S}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}
.

2)

\mathscr{I}^{+}
is endowed with a degenerate metric
q_{ab}:=\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{g}_{ab}
which satisfies
q_{ab}n^{a}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0
, where
n^{a}
is the null normal to
\mathscr{I}^{+}
.

3)

Moreover, in a divergence free conformal frame,
12
12

12

Fixing a conformal frame is akin to fixing a gauge. It is not a necessary step and everything can be done without choosing a special conformal frame, but this choice drastically simplifies computations.
the degenerate metric satisfies
\mathcal{L}_{n}q_{ab}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0
.

A pictorial representation of the universal structure is provided by Figure
9
. However, recall that working in a divergence-free conformal frame does not completely fix the conformal completion. There is a residual rescaling freedom and any given spacetime can be described by an equivalence class of conformal completions. That is, the pairs
(q_{ab},n^{a})
and
(q^{\prime}_{ab},n^{\prime a})
describe the same universal structure of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
, provided they are related to each other as
(q^{\prime}_{ab},n^{\prime a})=(\omega^{2}q_{ab},\omega^{-1}\,n^{a})
, where
\omega
has to satisfy
\mathcal{L}_{n}\omega\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0
and
\omega\neq 0
, and where the two conformal factors are related by
\Omega^{\prime}=\omega\,\Omega
.

Hence, the asymptotic symmetry group has to be defined in the following way: A diffeomorphism
d\in\textsf{Diff}(\mathscr{I}^{+})
is element of the
BMS group

\mathcal{B}
if there exists some
\omega
with
\mathcal{L}_{n}\omega=0
such that

\displaystyle d(q_{ab})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}\omega^{2}q_{ab}

\displaystyle d(n^{a})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}\omega^{-1}\,n^{a}.

(5.1)

Put differently, the symmetry group of the universal structure of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
consists of all diffeomorphisms
d
which do not necessarily leave the pair
(q_{ab},n^{a})
invariant (as this would be too restrictive), but which map
(q_{ab},n^{a})
to another pair which generates
the same
structure.

As an example, consider the diffeomorphism
d
which maps one generator of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
(i.e., an integral line) to another generator, as shown in Figure
10
. Notice that, because of the second equation in (
5
),
n^{a}
is mapped to a new vector proportional to
n^{a}
and hence the ruling of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
is preserved!

Figure

10
:
Visualization of the diffeomorphism which maps one integral line to an other one. This diffeomorphism clearly preserves the ruling of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
and thus its universal structure.

The asymptotic symmetry group
\mathcal{B}
is known as the
BMS group
, which is short for
Bondi-Metzner-Sachs group
. As alluded to in the introduction to this section, the BMS group is
larger
than the Poincaré group, which we would naïvely have expected to appear as the symmetry group of asymptotically Minkowski spacetimes, but instead it contains the Poincaré group as a subgroup. This fact is not at all obvious at this point, so let us investigate the structure of the BMS group and explicitly show its relation to the Poincaré group.

Note that in what follows, we do not write ’
\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}
’ explicitly anymore as the computations which follow are only meaningful on
\mathscr{I}^{+}
itself. We also adopt the following notation:

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle:\,\text{Symmetry group which preserves universal structure of $\mathscr{I}^{+}$ (BMS group)}

\displaystyle d(\lambda)

\displaystyle:\,\text{$1$-parameter family of diffeomorphisms which belong to $\mathcal{B}$ and with $d(0)=\textsf{id}$.}

Our next task is to study the “infinitesimal” generators of the BMS group. Recall that every
1
-parameter family of diffeomorphisms can be generated by a vector field. Let
b:=b^{a}\partial_{a}:=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}d(\lambda)\right|_{\lambda=0}
be the generating vector field of
d(\lambda)
on
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. These are the “infinitesimal” generators we seek to better understand. Moreover, observe that under a
1
-parameter family of diffeomorphisms
d(\lambda)
, the metric and the normal vector change according to
13
13

13

A comment on notation: The function
\omega
depends on the coordinates
\theta
,
\phi
and parameter
\lambda
, i.e.,
\omega(\theta,\phi,\lambda)
. We suppress the dependence on the angles and only display the dependence on the parameter
\lambda
, which is induced by the
1
-parameter family of diffeomorphisms,
d(\lambda)
.

\displaystyle\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{q}_{ab}

\displaystyle:=d(\lambda)^{*}q_{ab}=\omega(\lambda)^{2}\,q_{ab}

\displaystyle\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\rightharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{n}^{a}

\displaystyle:=d(\lambda)_{*}n^{a}=\omega(\lambda)^{-1}\,n^{a}.

(5.2)

Here,
d(\lambda)^{*}
and
d(\lambda)_{*}
denote the pullback and pushforward operations, respectively, and the right hand side of (
5
) follows from the definition of the BMS group. Hence, if we wish to study the generators
b^{a}
, we need to compare
\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{q}_{ab}
and
\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\rightharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{n}^{a}
to
q_{ab}
and
n^{a}
, respectively, for
\lambda
close to zero (which translates into
d(\lambda)
being close to the identity). To that end, it is convenient to introduce

\alpha(\theta,\phi):=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\omega(\lambda)\right|_{\lambda=0},

(5.3)

where it follows from
\mathcal{L}_{n}\omega\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0
that
\alpha
is only a function of
(\theta,\phi)
on
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. The “infinitesimal” action of
d(\lambda)
on the metric is then defined as

\displaystyle\underset{=\mathcal{L}_{b}q_{ab}}{\underbrace{\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\left(d(\lambda)^{*}q-q\right)_{ab}\right|_{\lambda=0}}}

\displaystyle=\underset{=2\alpha\,q_{ab}}{\underbrace{\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\left(\omega(\lambda)^{2}\,q-q\right)_{ab}\right|_{\lambda=0}}},

(5.4)

where we have used the definition of the Lie derivative. Similarly, for the normal vector one finds

\underset{=\mathcal{L}_{b}n^{a}}{\underbrace{\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\left(d(\lambda)_{*}n-n\right)^{a}\right|_{\lambda=0}}}=\underset{=-\alpha\,n^{a}}{\underbrace{\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\left(\omega(\lambda)^{-1}\,n-n\right)^{a}\right|_{\lambda=0}}}.

(5.5)

Thus, the “infinitesimal” diffeomorphisms of the BMS group are characterized as being generated by vector fields
b
which satisfy the conditions

\boxed{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{L}_{b}q_{ab}&=2\alpha\,q_{ab},\\
\mathcal{L}_{b}n^{a}&=-\alpha\,n^{a},\end{aligned}}

(5.6)

for some function
\alpha=\alpha(\theta,\phi)
. Let us denote the set of all vectors
b
which satisfy the above conditions by
\mathfrak{b}
(fraktur b) and the Lie bracket of vector fields by
[\cdot,\cdot]
. Is
(\mathfrak{b},[\cdot,\cdot],\mathbb{R})
a Lie algebra?

The answer is in the affirmative. All we need to show is that
\mathfrak{b}
is a real vector space and that
[\cdot,\cdot]:\mathfrak{b}\times\mathfrak{b}\to\mathfrak{b}
, i.e., that the Lie bracket maps any two vectors from
\mathfrak{b}
to some other vector in
\mathfrak{b}
. To show that
\mathfrak{b}
is a real vector space, all we need to show is that if
b_{1},b_{2}\in\mathfrak{b}
, then it follows that
\lambda_{1}b_{1}+\lambda_{2}b_{2}\in\mathfrak{b}
for all
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
. Indeed, we easily find that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\lambda_{1}b_{1}+\lambda_{2}b_{2}}q_{ab}

\displaystyle=\alpha\,q_{ab}

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\lambda_{1}b_{1}+\lambda_{2}b_{2}}n^{a}

\displaystyle=-\alpha\,n^{a},

(5.7)

with
\alpha=\alpha_{1}\lambda_{1}+\alpha_{2}\lambda_{2}
. Thus,
\mathfrak{b}
is a real vector space. Next, we need to show that the Lie bracket maps vectors from
\mathfrak{b}
on vectors in
\mathfrak{b}
, i.e., that it is an endomorphism on
\mathfrak{b}
. This translates into the question whether
[b_{1},b_{2}]
satisfies the conditions (
5.6
), provided
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
satisfy them. Using
\mathcal{L}_{[X,Y]}T=\mathcal{L}_{X}\mathcal{L}_{Y}T-\mathcal{L}_{Y}\mathcal{L}_{X}T
, which holds for any vector fields
X
,
Y
and any tensor field
T
, we immediately find

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{[b_{1},b_{2}]}q_{ab}

\displaystyle=2\alpha\,q_{ab}

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{[b_{1},b_{2}]}n^{a}

\displaystyle=-\alpha\,n^{a},

(5.8)

with
\alpha=\mathcal{L}_{b_{1}}\alpha_{2}-\mathcal{L}_{b_{2}}\alpha_{1}
. Hence, we have verified that
[\cdot,\cdot]:\mathfrak{b}\times\mathfrak{b}\to\mathfrak{b}
and we finally conclude that
(\mathfrak{b},[\cdot,\cdot],\mathbb{R})
forms a Lie algebra. Our next task is to better understand the structure of this Lie algebra and to provide an interpretation of the transformations it generates.

5.A
Supertranslations

We have derived the abstract conditions (
5.6
), which characterize the “infinitesimal” generators
b
, and we have shown that the set of these vectors,
\mathfrak{b}
, forms a real Lie algebra with respect to the Lie bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]
. Can we deduce a more explicit form for these generators? The answer is yes. An educated guess is that some of these generators are given by
b^{a}=\beta\,n^{a}
, where
\beta=\beta(u,\theta,\phi)
is some function on
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. This guess is motivated by the following observations: Minkowski space possesses four global Killing vector fields which correspond to spacetime translations and whose limit to
\mathscr{I}^{+}
has the form
\beta(\theta,\phi)\,n^{a}
(see Chapter
2
equations (
2.6
) and see also Side Note
1.B
). Hence, these fields are also symmetries of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
and necessarily satisfy the conditions (
5.6
). More generally, we know that the universal structure of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
demands that
\mathcal{L}_{n}q_{ab}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0
. Hence,
b^{a}=\beta\,n^{a}
is a good candidate for an “infinitesimal” symmetry generator. Indeed, by direct computation we find that
b^{a}=\beta\,n^{a}
satisfies the first condition in (
5.6
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\beta n}q_{ab}

\displaystyle=\beta\mathcal{L}_{n}q_{ab}+q_{ac}n^{c}\left(\mathcal{D}_{b}\beta\right)+q_{bc}n^{c}\left(\mathcal{D}_{a}\beta\right)=0,

(5.9)

where we have used that the universal structure of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
demands that
\mathcal{L}_{n}q_{ab}=0
and
q_{ab}n^{a}=0
. Notice also that
\mathcal{D}
is an arbitrary
14
14

14

We cannot use the Levi-Civita connection on
\mathscr{I}^{+}
because the metric
q_{ab}
is degenerate and hence the Levi-Civita connection, which requires the inverse of
q_{ab}
, is not defined. Hence, there is not a canonical choice of connection on
\mathscr{I}^{+}
, but any choice is admissible here since neither the covariant derivative of scalars nor the Lie derivative depend on the connection.
covariant derivative operator on
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. It follows that
b^{a}=\beta\,n^{a}
satisfies the first condition in (
5.6
) with
\alpha=0
, in agreement with our educated guess.

Next, let us consider the second condition in (
5.6
), using the fact that
\alpha=0
, which we have just derived. A direct computation yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\beta n}n^{a}

\displaystyle=\beta n^{b}\mathcal{D}_{b}n^{a}-n^{c}\mathcal{D}_{c}\left(\beta n^{a}\right)

\displaystyle=\beta n^{b}\mathcal{D}_{b}n^{a}-n^{c}n^{a}\mathcal{D}_{c}\beta-n^{c}\beta\mathcal{D}_{c}n^{a}

\displaystyle=-n^{c}n^{a}\mathcal{D}_{c}\beta\overset{!}{=}0.

(5.10)

We end up with the condition that
n^{a}\mathcal{D}_{a}\beta\overset{!}{=}0
, which obviously tells us that
\beta
is independent of
u
. Thus, we have shown that
b^{a}=\beta\,n^{a}
, with
\beta=\beta(\theta,\phi)
, is an “infinitesimal” generator of the BMS group. In fact, we have found infinitely many generators since
\beta
is an arbitrary function. This makes
\mathfrak{b}
an
infinite-dimensional
Lie algebra.

Let us consider what happens if we change the conformal frame. That is, we assume that
(q_{ab},n^{a})
satisfy the conditions (
5.6
) for some
b^{a}=\beta\,n^{a}
and then we perform a conformal rescaling
(q_{ab},n^{a})\mapsto(q^{\prime}_{ab},n^{\prime a})=(\omega^{2}q_{ab},\omega^{-1}n^{a})
. It is easy to show that
b^{\prime a}=\beta^{\prime}\,n^{\prime a}
is an “infinitesimal” generator for the conformally rescaled pair
(q^{\prime}_{ab},n^{\prime a})
. However, if we want our considerations to be
independent
of the conformal frame we are operating in, we need to demand that

\displaystyle\beta\,n^{a}=b^{a}\overset{!}{=}b^{\prime}=\beta^{\prime}\,n^{\prime a}=\beta^{\prime}(\omega^{-1}n^{a}),

(5.11)

which implies that
\beta^{\prime}=\omega\beta
. In other words,
\beta
is not a “true” function. Rather, it is a scalar quantity which, under the residual conformal rescaling in a divergence-free conformal frame, transforms as
\beta^{\prime}=\omega\,\beta
. We say that
\beta
has
conformal weight

\mathbf{+1}
, while
n^{a}
is said to have
conformal weight

\mathbf{-1}
. This ensures that
b^{\prime a}=b^{a}
, i.e., that the symmetry generators are invariant under conformal rescaling.

Let us continue exploring the generators of the form
b^{a}=\beta\,n^{a}
. We already know that
\mathfrak{b}
, the set of
all
generators, is a Lie algebra. It is thus natural to ask whether the set
\mathfrak{s}
(fraktur s) of all
b^{a}=\beta\,n^{a}
forms a subalgebra of
\mathfrak{b}
. Let us recall that a subalgebra
\mathfrak{s}\subseteq\mathfrak{b}
is a subspace (in the sense of vector spaces), which is closed under the action of the Lie bracket. The latter requirement means that for all
s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathfrak{s}
, we have
[s_{1},s_{2}]\in\mathfrak{s}
.

To emphasize that we are considering a special set of vectors, let us change notation and denote
\beta\,n^{a}
by
s^{a}:=\beta\,n^{a}
, where
\beta=\beta(\theta,\phi)
. Furthermore, let
\mathfrak{s}
be the set of all these vectors
s^{a}
. It is easy to see that when
s_{1}\in\mathfrak{s}
and
s_{2}\in\mathfrak{s}
, then it follows that
\lambda_{1}s_{1}+\lambda_{2}s_{2}\in\mathfrak{s}
for all
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
. Hence,
\mathfrak{s}
forms a vector space and this is a subspace of the vector space
\mathfrak{b}
. For the Lie bracket, we find for
s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathfrak{s}

\displaystyle[s_{1},s_{2}]^{a}

\displaystyle=s^{b}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{b}s^{a}_{2}-s^{b}_{2}\mathcal{D}_{b}s^{a}_{1}=\beta_{1}n^{b}\mathcal{D}_{b}(\beta_{2}n^{a})-\beta_{2}n^{b}\mathcal{D}_{b}(\beta_{1}n^{a})

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}n^{b}\mathcal{D}_{b}n^{a}-\beta_{1}\beta_{2}n^{b}\mathcal{D}_{b}n^{a}}_{=0}+\beta_{1}n^{a}\underbrace{n^{b}\mathcal{D}_{b}\beta_{2}}_{=0}-\beta_{2}n^{a}\underbrace{n^{b}\mathcal{D}_{b}\beta_{1}}_{=0}

\displaystyle=0,

(5.12)

where we have used that
n^{a}\mathcal{D}_{a}\beta=0
. Because
0\in\mathfrak{s}
, it follows that
\mathfrak{s}
is closed under the action of the Lie bracket. Thus,
\mathbf{\mathbf{}}{\mathfrak{s}}

is a subalgebra of

\mathbf{\mathbf{}}{\mathfrak{b}}
. Moreover, because
[s_{1},s_{2}]=0
for all
s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathfrak{s}
, it is an
abelian Lie algebra
.

Let us briefly summarize the situation thus far: The set of “infinitesimal” generators of the BMS group, i.e., the vectors
b
which satisfy (
5.6
), form a real, infinite-dimensional Lie algebra
\mathfrak{b}
. This Lie algebra admits a real, infinite dimensional abelian Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{s}
, which is defined by

\displaystyle s^{a}:=\beta\,n^{a}\in\mathfrak{s}\quad\text{with}\quad\mathcal{L}_{n}\beta=0.

(5.13)

We call this subalgebra the algebra of
supertranslations
(hence the use of the letters
s
and
\mathfrak{s}
). This is motivated by the fact that our educated guess —that
\beta\,n^{a}
are “infinitesimal” generators— originated from the observation that the translational Killing vector fields of Minkowski space have the form
\beta(\theta,\phi)n^{a}
when we take their limit to
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. Moreover, we also know that the Poincaré Lie algebra admits an abelian subalgebra and this algebra is precisely the algebra of spacetime translations. However, there is an important difference: In the Poincaré case, the subalgebra is
finite-dimensional
, while we found an
infinite-dimensional
subalgebra in the BMS case. This is the reason why we call it the algebra of
super
translations and we will see that this enlargement of the algebra —from “ordinary” translations to supertranslations— has important consequences.

We will shortly see that there are further parallels between the Poincaré Lie algebra and the Lie algebra of the BMS group. To make this more precise, let us denote the Lie algebra of the Poincaré group by the symbol
15
15

15

In French,
point
means point and
carré
means square. Put together,
point-carré
sounds like
Poincaré
and therefore we use the symbol
\boxdot
to represent this Lie algebra.

\boxdot
, and the subalgebra of translations by
\mathfrak{t}
(fraktur t). It turns out that
\mathfrak{t}
is not just a subalgebra of
\boxdot
, it is a so-called
ideal
. This means that the Lie bracket between any element
p\in\boxdot
and any element
t\in\mathfrak{t}
lies again in
\mathfrak{t}
, i.e.,
[p,t]\in\mathfrak{t}
for all
p\in\boxdot
and all
t\in\mathfrak{t}
. As we will explain in more detail in the next subsection, this property guarantees that the quotient of
\boxdot
and
\mathfrak{t}
, i.e., the space
\boxdot/\mathfrak{t}
, is (a) well-defined and (b) again a Lie algebra. In fact, one finds that

\boxdot/\mathfrak{t}\simeq\mathfrak{l},

(5.14)

where
\mathfrak{l}
(fraktur l) is the six-dimensional Lie algebra of Lorentz transformations (three rotations and three boosts). Given this fact, it is natural to inquire whether the supertranslations
\mathfrak{s}
also form an ideal of
\mathfrak{b}
. This would then allow us to construct the quotient space
\mathfrak{b}/\mathfrak{s}
and investigate its relation with the Lie algebra of Lorentz transformations. To check whether
\mathfrak{s}
is an ideal of
\mathfrak{b}
, we only need to verify that
[b,s]\in\mathfrak{s}
for all
b\in\mathfrak{b}
and all
s\in\mathfrak{s}
. A direct computation yields

\displaystyle[b,\beta\,n]^{a}=\mathcal{L}_{b}(\beta n^{a})=n^{a}\mathcal{L}_{b}\beta+\beta\underbrace{\mathcal{L}_{b}n^{a}}_{=-\alpha\,n^{a}}=n^{a}\mathcal{L}_{b}\beta-\beta\alpha\,n^{a}=\underbrace{(\mathcal{L}_{b}\beta-\beta\alpha)}_{=:\beta^{\prime}}\,n^{a}=:s^{\prime a},

(5.15)

where we used condition (
5.6
) to rewrite the Lie derivative of
n^{a}
along
b
. We find that the final output of the above computation is a vector in
\mathfrak{s}
, that is, we find a supertranslation vector again. Hence, the
supertranslations form an ideal of

\mathfrak{b}
. In the next subsection, we will briefly review some important mathematical concepts, which will help us in fully appreciating the importance of the result we just derived. Readers familiar with equivalence relations, kernels, Lie algebra homomorphisms, kernels, and quotient groups can skip the mathematical interlude or read it as a reminder of certain definitions.

5.A.1
Interlude: Important Mathematical Concepts

Our goal is to understand how the ideal
\mathfrak{i}
of a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
lead to a well-defined quotient space
\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{i}
and how this space has to be understood. To that end, we recall some important mathematical concepts.

Equivalence Relations:

Let
S
be a set. A
relation

\sim
on
S
is some way of relating elements of
S
to each other. For instance, if
S
is the set of your family members,
x\sim y
could mean “
x=y
(
x
and
y
are the same person) or
x
is a brother or sister or
y
” and
x\approx y
, a different relation defined on
S
, might mean “
x
is the mother of
y
”. Both
x\sim y
and
x\approx y
define a relation on
S
.

Here, we are only interested in a special class of relations. We say that a relation
\sim
is an
equivalence relation
if it possesses the following properties for all
x,y,z
in
S
:

\displaystyle\textsf{1) }x\sim x

(Reflexivity)

2)
If
x\sim y
