\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0.

(6.41)

2)

Since
n_{a}n^{a}=0
,
n_{a}
is tangent to
\mathcal{N}
. As such,
n^{a}
is geodesic and satisfies the geodesic equation

\displaystyle n^{a}\nabla_{a}n^{b}=\kappa_{(n)}n^{b},

(6.42)

where
\kappa_{(n)}
is the
surface gravity
of
\mathcal{N}
with respect to the specific choice of
n^{a}
.
23
23

23

It is important to remember that when translated into a thermodynamical point of view, the surface gravity can be understood as an analogue of temperature. Thus, when entering the realm of thermodynamics, the freedom in the choice of
n^{a}
has to be removed somehow as otherwise it would result in an ambiguous definition of surface gravity and hence temperature.

We can further introduce a basis on each tangent space
T_{p}\mathcal{N}
with
p\in\mathcal{N}
. For a given null normal
n^{a}
we can always choose two tangent vectors
v,w
such that
\{n,v,w\}
form a basis of
T_{p}\mathcal{N}
for all
p
. Since
\mathcal{N}
is null, every tangent vector to
\mathcal{N}
must either be spacelike or null itself (we gave an intuition for this subtlety already in a side note in section
1.B
). Let us first chose both tangent vectors to be spacelike and normalized in a “standard” sense, i.e.,
v^{a}w_{a}=0
and
v^{a}v_{a}=w^{a}w_{a}=1
. Then, the spacelike subspace spanned by
\{v,w\}
has two null normals, one of which is chosen to be
n^{a}
. We denote the other null normal by
\ell^{a}
and normalize such that
n^{a}\ell_{a}=-1
. The term “null normal” has to be used with caution here. That is, we have to differentiate between null normals to
\mathcal{N}
and null normals to the
2
-dimensional subspace spanned by
v,w
: The null surface
\mathcal{N}
has only one null normal which we denoted as
n^{a}
here, the vector
\ell^{a}
is a null normal only with respect to the
\{v,w\}
-subspace. Note at this point that while
n^{a}
is inward-pointing,
\ell^{a}
is an outward-pointing null normal. Altogether, the vectors
\{n,\ell,v,w\}
define a natural basis of
T_{p}\mathcal{M}
. For our purposes, it is convenient to choose a more “familiar” basis for
T_{p}\mathcal{M}
namely a basis in which the tangent vectors to
\mathcal{N}
are also null. This way we obtain the known null tetrad
\{n,\ell,m,\bar{m}\}
, in literature often named
Newman-Penrose basis
. All we have to do is define

\displaystyle m\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(v+iw)

and

\displaystyle\bar{m}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(v-iw).

(6.43)

It follows that
m
and
\bar{m}
automatically satisfy
m^{a}m_{a}=\bar{m}^{a}\bar{m}_{a}=0
and
m^{a}\bar{m}_{a}=\bar{m}^{a}m_{a}=1
. It also holds that
\spn\{v,w\}=\spn\{m,\bar{m}\}
and consequently
\{n,\ell,m,\bar{m}\}
establishes an alternative basis for
T_{p}\mathcal{M}
.

Carrying on, we can define an intrinsic metric
q_{ab}
of
\mathcal{N}
as the pullback of the spacetime metric
g_{ab}

\displaystyle q_{ab}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{g}_{ab},

(6.44)

which is degenerate as
q_{ab}n^{a}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{g}_{ab}n^{a}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{n}_{a}=0
, i.e.,
n^{a}
is the degenerate direction of
q_{ab}
. The signature of
q_{ab}
is
(0,+,+)
and its determinant vanishes. As discussed already in the context of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
, an inverse can be imposed here anyways by its defining equation

\displaystyle q^{ab}q_{ac}q_{bd}=q_{cd}.

(6.45)

For
\mathcal{N}
, we find a freedom of choice in the inverse given by
\tilde{q}^{ab}=q^{ab}+n^{(a}X^{b)}
where
X^{a}
is tangent to
\mathcal{N}
. Both
\tilde{q}^{ab}
and
q^{ab}
satisfy the inverse equation (
6.45
).

It is well known that the full spacetime metric of this interlude,
g_{ab}
, can be constructed solely from the tetrad we derived here. In fact, in Exercises
1.2
it was shown that
g_{ab}=-2\ell_{(a}n_{b)}-2m_{(a}m_{b)}=-2\ell_{(a}n_{b)}+q_{ab}
, where
q_{ab}
is the metric on our hypersurface
\mathcal{N}
. As
g_{ab}
is non-degenerate, we are guaranteed to find a unique covariant derivative
\nabla
on full spacetime such that
\nabla_{a}g_{bc}=0
and
\nabla
is torsion free.

This, unfortunately, is not the case for the null surface
\mathcal{N}
. Its intrinsic metric
q_{ab}
is degenerate, prohibiting the uniqueness of a covariant derivative operator
\mathcal{D}
on
\mathcal{N}
. It is impossible to pick out a preferred and well-defined derivative operator on the null surface if we do not impose additional conditions on
\mathcal{N}
.
24
24

24

“Well-defined” here, as above, has to be equated with metric-compatible and torsion-free.

The difficulties of defining a derivative operator on
\mathcal{N}
are exactly equivalent to the ones we are facing when encountering derivative operators defined at
\mathscr{I}
. Furthermore, many definitions (such as the tetrads and the inverse metric) made here admit direct analogues in the context of
\mathscr{I}
. For the null surface
\mathcal{N}
, we will solve issues arising with the definition of the derivative operator partially by using NEHs, and, as suggested above, we can do so for
\mathscr{I}^{+}
as well.

Any null hypersurface defined in similar fashion as
\mathcal{N}
can be cast into a NEH by imposing additional properties. For that purpose, we will keep the notation of the spacetime metric
g_{ab}
and of the metric on the null hypersurface
q_{ab}
, as well as of the derivative on general spacetime
\nabla
. As the name suggests, we define NEH to be horizons in an
equilibrium state
. In the weakest form, this translates to

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{n}q_{ab}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\underleftarrow{\nabla_{(a}n_{b)}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0

(6.46)

since
\mathcal{L}_{n}q_{ab}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}\mathcal{L}_{n}\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{g}_{ab}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}2\underleftarrow{\nabla_{(a}n_{b)}}
. We can interpret the former statement as
\mathcal{N}
’s intrinsic geometry to be invariant under time translations which is the very definition of being “static” or in “equilibrium”. This becomes more apparent when we rewrite the null normal of the hypersurface,
n^{a}
, and the Lie derivative in its direction,
\mathcal{L}_{n}
, in terms of an affine parameter
u
which, based on the coordinate chart
\{t,r,\theta,\phi\}
, can be identified with the retarded time coordinate. Note that we will
not impose
equation (
6.46
) for NEHs but we will show that it naturally follows from their definition. For completeness, note also that
\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{\nabla_{a}\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{n}_{b}}
can be decomposed into a
trace
which is commonly called
expansion
of
n^{a}
, here denoted as
\theta_{(n)}
, and a
symmetric trace-free part
called
shear
of
n^{a}
, here
\sigma_{ab}
. They read

\displaystyle\theta_{(n)}

\displaystyle=q^{ab}\nabla_{a}n_{b},

\displaystyle\sigma_{ab}

\displaystyle=\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{\nabla_{(a}n_{b)}}-q_{ab}a^{cd}\nabla_{c}n_{d}.

(6.47)

Both vanish if
\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{\nabla_{a}\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{n}_{b}}
is zero and are independent of the choice of the inverse metric, as will be shown in Exercise
6.1
. As the trace of
\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{\nabla_{a}\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{n}_{b}}
is part of the definition of NEHs we now have everything at hand to give a precise description.

Definition 6.1: Non-expanding horizon

A three-dimensional hypersurface
\Delta\subset\mathcal{M}
of a space time
(\mathcal{M},g_{ab})
is said to be a
non-expanding horizon
if it satisfies the following conditions:

1)

\Delta
is topologically
\mathbb{S}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}
.

2)

The expansion
\theta_{(n)}
vanishes for any null normal
n^{\prime a}=fn^{a}
.

3)

Einstein’s field equations hold on
\Delta
.

4)

The energy-momentum tensor
T_{ab}
of external matter fields is such that at
\Delta
, the contraction
-T^{a}_{b}n^{b}
is future directed for any null normal.

Let us take a moment to digest this definition and remark on a few points. First, from now on we focus on NEHs instead of “normal” null hypersurfaces
\mathcal{N}
, hence, we switch the notation of our hypersurface from
\mathcal{N}
to
\Delta
. Secondly, note that in this framework with the above equilibrium condition,
n^{a}
is hypersurface orthogonal. In Exercise
6.6
it is shown that this has far reaching implications and one can conclude that every null normal of
\Delta
Lie drags the metric
q_{ab}
intrinsic to
\Delta
.

Let us assume now the hypersurface we are considering in this interlude is indeed a NEH and all the properties we derived so far hold. We denote our null surface as
\Delta
and construct two vectors
X^{a},Y^{a}
tangent to
\Delta
. For null surfaces in general, we do not have a natural way to pull back the full spacetime derivative operator
\nabla
to obtain an derivative on the hypersurface. For NEHs, however, there is a well-defined way. What we mean by “well-defined” boils down to the core principles of the derivative operator. That is, we want to find an operator
\mathcal{D}=\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{\nabla}
satisfying the three axioms of a derivative operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{a}(X^{b}+Y^{b})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}_{a}X^{b}+\mathcal{D}_{a}Y^{b},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{a}(fX^{b})

\displaystyle=f\mathcal{D}_{a}X^{b}+X^{b}\mathcal{D}_{a}f,

\displaystyle X^{a}\mathcal{D}_{a}f

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}_{X}f.

(6.48)

Note that in the axioms we consider vectors
X,Y
tangent to
\Delta
as the derivative operator we aim for is intrinsic to
\Delta
. Hence, the tangent spaces of
\Delta
are the vector spaces it is applied to. If we work through the axioms equipped with the machinery of the NEH-toolbox we find that the well-definiteness of the derivative operator boils down to the following statement: If
Y^{a}\nabla_{a}X^{b}
is tangent to
\Delta
then
\mathcal{D}_{a}=\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{\nabla_{a}}
is well-defined. Using the result of Exercise
6.6
, namely that
n
is twist-free, and
\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{\nabla_{(a}}\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{n}_{b)}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0
, we find
\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{\nabla_{a}}\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{n}_{b}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0
. Then, for
Y_{a}n^{a}=X_{a}n^{a}=0
, i.e., two vectors
X^{a},Y^{a}
tangent to
\Delta
we find

\displaystyle Y^{b}\nabla_{b}(X^{a}n_{a})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}Y^{b}X^{a}\nabla_{b}n_{a}+Y^{b}n_{a}\nabla_{b}X^{a}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}Y^{b}X^{a}\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{\nabla_{b}n_{a}}+n_{a}Y^{b}\nabla_{b}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}l_{a}Y^{b}\nabla_{b}X^{a}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0,

(6.49)

which means that
Y^{a}\nabla_{a}X^{b}
is truly tangent to the hypersurface
\Delta
.
Consequently,
\mathcal{D}
is defined by
\mathcal{D}_{a}X^{b}=\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{\nabla_{a}}\tilde{X}^{b}
, where
\tilde{X}^{b}
is an arbitrary expansion of
X^{b}
to full spacetime. It is left as an exercise to show that the definition is independent of the extension. The above definition of the derivative operator intrinsic to
\Delta
can be applied to co-vectors and, subsequently, tensors of arbitrary type on
\Delta
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{e}T^{a...b}{}_{c...d}=\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{\nabla_{e}\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{T}^{a...b}{}_{c...d}}.

(6.50)

Most importantly, the derivative operator
\mathcal{D}
is metric-compatible with respect to the intrinsic metric of the NEH
\Delta
, i.e.,
\mathcal{D}_{a}q_{bc}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}\nabla_{a}g_{bc}
.

It seems like we reached the goal of this interlude. Before we turn back to our null infinity hypersurface
\mathscr{I}^{+}
, though, another remark is in order. The intrinsic derivative of our null normal
n^{a}
can be shown to have a “special” form. To do so, we first notice that since
X^{a}Y^{b}\nabla_{a}n_{b}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0
it follows that
X^{a}\nabla_{a}n_{b}\sim n_{b}
. Capturing the proportionality to
X^{a}
of the latter expression, we conclude that
X^{a}\nabla_{a}n_{b}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}X^{a}\omega_{a}n_{b}
where we define
\omega_{a}
as the
induced normal connection
determined by
\mathcal{D}
and
n^{a}
. Then, it holds that

\displaystyle\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{\nabla_{a}}n_{b}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}\mathcal{D}_{a}n_{b}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}\omega_{a}n_{b}

(6.51)

and one can show that based on this definition,
\mathcal{L}_{n}\omega_{a}=0=\mathcal{L}_{\ell}\omega_{a}
.

Let us summarize the results so far. We found that a null surface has no unique derivative operator due to the degeneracy of the intrinsic metric and the pullback of the derivative operator from general spacetime to the hypersurface is only well-defined if we impose additional constraints on the null surface. That is, if we make it a NEH. As mentioned earlier, for us this is a very convenient observation as null infinity in a conformally completed spacetime is mathematically a NEH. Hence, we can use what we derived here regarding the operator
\mathcal{D}
in order to obtain a well-defined derivative operator on
\mathscr{I}
.

6.E
Exercises

Exercise 6.1

Let
(\mathcal{N},q_{ab})
be a co-dimension one null hypersurface embedded in
(\mathcal{M},g_{ab})
and let
n^{a}
be the null normal to
\mathcal{N}
. Denote the torsion-free and metric-compatible covariant derivative on
(\mathcal{M},g_{ab})
by
\nabla
. The pullback
\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{\nabla_{a}n_{b}}
(from
\mathcal{M}
to
\mathcal{N}
) can be decomposed into a trace and a symmetric trace-free part as

\displaystyle\theta_{(n)}

\displaystyle:=q^{ab}\nabla_{a}n_{b},

\displaystyle\sigma_{ab}

\displaystyle:=\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{\nabla_{(a}n_{b)}}-q_{ab}a^{cd}\nabla_{c}n_{d},

where
q^{ab}
is a pseudo-inverse. Prove that
\theta_{(n)}
and
\sigma^{(n)}_{ab}
are independent of the choice of the pseudo-inverse, i.e., they are invariant under
q^{ab}\mapsto\tilde{q}^{ab}=q^{ab}+n^{(a}X^{b)}
, where
X^{b}
is any vector tangent to
\mathcal{N}
.

Exercise 6.2

Let
\omega_{a}
be the induced normal connection of a non-expanding horizon (NEH). Compute its behavior under transformations
n^{a}\mapsto n^{\prime a}=fn^{a}
and find an explicit expression for the surface gravity
\kappa_{(n)}
of a NEH in terms of
n^{a}
and
\omega_{a}
.

Hint: Use equation (
6.51
).

Exercise 6.3

Prove the transformation law (
6.21
) in Minkowski space for a displacement vector
d^{a}
leading from an origin
O
to
O^{\prime}
.

Exercise 6.4

Define
\mathcal{D}:=\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{\nabla}
. In this chapter it was shown that the action of this operator on scalars
f
and
1
-forms
\omega_{a}
is well-defined. Show that if the action on
1
-forms is known, we also know how
\mathcal{D}
acts on vector fields
v^{a}
tangent to
\mathscr{I}^{+}
, even when there is no well-defined inverse metric to raise indices.

Hint: Consider
q_{bc}n^{c}\mathcal{D}_{a}v^{b}
and
\omega_{b}\mathcal{D}_{a}v^{b}
.

Exercise 6.5

Let
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
be two differential operators which satisfy the properties (
6.32
). Furthermore, let
m^{a}
be part of a Newman-Penrose null tetrad. Show that

\left(\mathcal{D}-\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\right)\alpha_{b}=0,

(6.52)

where
\alpha_{b}=f\,m_{a}+\bar{f}\,\bar{m}_{a}
.

Exercise 6.6

Let
n^{a}
be the null normal of a NEH as defined in the interlude on NEHs
6.D.1
. Prove that

\displaystyle\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{\nabla}_{(a}\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{n}_{b)}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0

and hence

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{n}q_{ab}=0.

Hint: Prove first that
n^{a}
is shear free using that it is hypersurface orthogonal and thus twist-free. Also, use the Raychaudhuri equation.

Exercise 6.7

Show that if
n^{a}
is a null normal such that
q_{ab}n^{b}=0
and
\mathcal{L}_{n}q_{ab}=0
, then both equations hold for all null normals
n^{\prime a}=fn^{a}
, where
f
is any smooth function.

Hint: Use the Cartan identity.

Chapter 7: Radiative Modes in Full, Non-Linear General Relativity

Let us briefly recapitulate the story so far. We have argued that the derivative operator on null infinity incorporates information about the radiative modes of the gravitational field in the bulk. We engaged on a search for a well-defined derivative operator on
\mathscr{I}^{+}
and found that there is not one, but many such operators. In fact, any torsion-free and metric-compatible operator which satisfies
\mathcal{D}_{a}n^{b}=0
is admissible.
In an attempt to quantify the ambiguity, we analyzed the operator’s action on
1
-forms
\alpha_{a}
transverse to
n^{a}
. In doing so, we found that all derivative operators on
\mathscr{I}^{+}
have the same action on such
1
-forms. That is, any two derivative operators
D
,
D^{\prime}
which are torsion-free, metric compatible, and which satisfy
\mathcal{D}_{a}n^{b}=0=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{a}n^{b}
satisfy

\left(\mathcal{D}_{a}-\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{a}\right)\alpha_{b}=0.

(7.1)

However, the actions of
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
on
\ell_{a}
, which is a
1
-form which is
not
transverse to
n^{a}
, are generally different. We have argued that this is where radiative modes will be revealed. It is this “non-universality” of the action of
\mathcal{D}
on
\ell_{a}
which allows us to distinguish between spacetimes with and without radiation.

In this chapter, we will make this notion more precise by the introduction of equivalence classes of derivative operators. We will also “count” how many operators there are. We will find that each derivative operator possesses three degrees of freedom, in a sense which will become clear in the next subsection. One degree of freedom is pure “gauge” and corresponds to a choice of conformal completion. The remaining two degrees of freedom encode the radiative modes of the gravitational field.

7.A
Equivalence Classes of Derivative Operators on Null Infinity

Let
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
be two distinct, torsion-free, and metric-compatible covariant derivative operators on
\mathscr{I}^{+}
which satisfy
\mathcal{D}_{a}n^{b}=0=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{a}n^{b}
. Furthermore, let
\alpha_{b}
be
any

1
-form on the co-tangent space of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. As is well-known from differential geometry, the difference between any two covariant derivative operators is a tensor. Thus, we can write

\displaystyle(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{a}-\mathcal{D}_{a})\alpha_{b}=C_{ab}{}^{c}\alpha_{c},

(7.2)

where
C_{ab}{}^{c}=C_{(ab)}{}^{c}
is the tensor we alluded to above. Notice that it has to be symmetric because the connections used to construct
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
are torsion-free. We know from the last chapter that the action of
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
on
1
-forms
\omega_{a}
which are transverse to
\mathscr{I}^{+}
, i.e., which satisfy
\omega_{a}n^{a}
, is universal in the sense that
\left(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{a}-\mathcal{D}_{a}\right)\omega_{b}=0
. From this we conclude

\displaystyle\underbrace{(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{a}-\mathcal{D}_{a})\omega_{b}}_{=0}

\displaystyle=C_{ab}{}^{c}\omega_{c}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle C_{ab}{}^{c}=\Sigma_{ab}n^{c}.

(7.3)

In words: we can conclude that the tensor
C_{ab}{}^{c}
is the product of a symmetric tensor
\Sigma_{ab}
and the null normal
n^{c}
. By its very construction, this ensures that
C_{ab}{}^{c}\omega_{c}=0
for all transversal
1
-forms. In addition to that, we also know that
\mathcal{D}_{a}n^{b}=0=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{a}n^{b}
, from which we conclude

\displaystyle\underbrace{(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{a}-\mathcal{D}_{a})n^{b}}_{=0}=C_{ac}{}^{b}n^{c}

\displaystyle=\left(\Sigma_{ac}n^{c}\right)n^{b}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\Sigma_{ac}n^{c}=0.

(7.4)

It follows that
\Sigma_{ab}
is transverse to
n^{a}
. From these findings we deduce that our freedom in choosing a derivative operator on
\mathscr{I}^{+}
is reduced to choosing a symmetric tensor which is transverse to
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. Put differently, there are as many distinct covariant derivative operators on
\mathscr{I}^{+}
as there are tensors of this type. How many such tensors are there?

Given that
\Sigma_{ab}
is a rank-
2
tensor which is defined on a three-dimensional manifold, it has
3\times 3
components. However, it is symmetric and this reduces the number of independent components to
\frac{3(3+1)}{2}=6
. Taking into account the transversality, which imposes the three constraint equations
\Sigma_{ab}n^{b}=0
, finally leaves us with
three
independent components which completely specify
\Sigma_{ab}
.

This is not the end of the story, though. We have to keep in mind that we work in a fixed divergence-free conformal frame
(q_{ab},n^{a})
. However, there is nothing special about the choice we made and someone else might have chosen a
different
divergence-free conformal frame
(q^{\prime ab},n^{\prime a})
for the same spacetime. We recall that any two such frames are related by a conformal transformation of the form
(q_{ab},n^{a})\mapsto(q^{\prime ab},n^{\prime a})=(\omega^{2}q^{ab},\omega^{-1}\,n^{a})
, where
\omega
is a smooth, nowhere vanishing function which is Lie dragged by
n^{a}
. How does this rescaling freedom affect our covariant derivative operator?
Notice that fixing a divergence-free conformal frame is akin of a partial “gauge fixing” in the following sense: We are free in choosing a conformal completion of the physical spacetime,
(\hat{\mathcal{M}},\hat{g}_{ab})\mapsto(\mathcal{M},g_{ab},\Omega)
. However, as we have spelled out in Chapter
3
, there is a canonical choice for a conformal completion and we can always make that choice. Of course, we are talking about a divergence-free conformal frame. Hence, among all the possible “gauge” choices
\Omega
we have selected one for which
\nabla_{a}n^{a}
holds. However, this choice does not completely fix the “gauge” because there are infinitely many
\Omega
’s which lead to divergence-freeness and these
\Omega
’s are related by the residual rescaling freedom described above. Thus, we have not fixed a “gauge”, but rather a “gauge class”. This means that we actually have an equivalence class of derivative operators
\mathcal{D}
. Namely, given a
\mathcal{D}
which satisfies all the conditions spelled out above, we can generate a new operator simply by a conformal rescaling, thus obtaining an equivalence class
[\mathcal{D}]
in this sense that
\mathcal{D}\sim\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
if and only if they are related by a conformal rescaling. Observe that this is consistent with what we said in
6.D
about
\nabla
being determined by
\hat{g}_{ab}
(the
physical
metric) and
\Omega
. Thus, in a sense, when we pull back
\nabla
to
\mathscr{I}^{+}
in order to define
\mathcal{D}
, this intrinsic derivative operator “knows” about
\Omega
. But this
\Omega
is a “gauge artifact”. Thus, we anticipate that one of the three degrees of freedom in
\Sigma_{ab}
actually just represent the freedom to choose a conformal frame, leaving us with only two degrees of freedom which can be traced back to
\hat{g}_{ab}
. These are the two true degrees of freedom of the gravitational field and they represent the radiative modes!

This is the qualitative picture and it is somewhat hand-waving in certain places. So let us make everything precise and demonstrate that this picture is actually correct. To do so, we perform a conformal rescaling under the assumption that
\omega\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}1
, but
\omega\neq 1
off
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. It obviously follows that the universal structure of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
is invariant under this particular transformation:

\displaystyle q^{\prime}_{ab}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}q_{ab},

and

\displaystyle n^{\prime a}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}n^{a}.

However, as we have hopefully made clear in the last and in this chapter, the covariant derivative
\mathcal{D}
is
not
fully determined by the universal structure. In a sense, it probes the structure of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
“one level deeper”. More precisely,
\mathcal{D}
is not oblivious to what happens in a neighborhood of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
and therefore is affected by this particular transformation. Put in yet a different way, even when
\omega\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}1
this does
not
imply that the derivative of
\omega
off
\mathscr{I}^{+}
is trivial (i.e., it does not vanish) and hence it
does
affect the derivative operator
\mathcal{D}
.

To show that the action of
\mathcal{D}
is altered by the above rescaling transformation, we step away from
\mathscr{I}^{+}
into the conformally completed spacetime. There, we consider the derivative operator
\nabla
. As we know very well be now, this operator changes under a conformal rescaling to
\nabla^{\prime}
, and the two operators are related by (see Exercise
7.1
):

\displaystyle(\nabla^{\prime}_{a}-\nabla_{a})\alpha_{b}

\displaystyle=C_{ab}{}^{c}\alpha_{c}

with

\displaystyle C_{ab}{}^{c}

\displaystyle=-\omega^{-1}\left(2\delta^{c}{}_{(a}\nabla_{b)}\omega-(\nabla^{c}\omega)g_{ab}\right).

(7.5)

We can use this result to relate the derivative operators
\mathcal{D}:=\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{\nabla}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}:=\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{\nabla}^{\prime}
on
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. At this point, a word of caution is in order when performing this pullback operation.

Side Note 7.1:
The meaning of “pulling back free indices”

When considering composite expressions with many indices, where some of them are fully contracted, one has to be careful with the pullback operation. In such a case we say, maybe somewhat unorthodoxically, that we “pull back the free indices”. This terminology is probably best explained with an example: Assume we are given a tensor
Q_{ab}
which can be written as
(\nabla_{c}v^{c})h_{ab}
. The pullback of this tensor is then given by
(\nabla_{c}v^{c})\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{h}_{ab}
, where it is understood that the scalar
\nabla_{c}v^{c}
is restricted to the manifold onto which we are pulling back. But no additional pullback operation on individual tensors, i.e., something like
\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{\nabla_{c}v^{c}}
or
\nabla_{c}\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{v^{c}}
, is necessary. In this sense, the dummy indices remain untouched and only the free indices
a
and
b
are pulled back.

With this clarification out of the way, we finally compute the pullback of
(\nabla^{\prime}_{a}-\nabla_{a})\alpha_{b}
to
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. Taking care to only pull back the free indices
a
and
b
, this leads to

\displaystyle\underleftarrow{(\nabla^{\prime}_{a}-\nabla_{a})\alpha_{b}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{a}-\mathcal{D}_{a})\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{\alpha_{b}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}-\omega^{-1}\left(2[\delta^{c}{}\underleftarrow{\prescript{}{(a}{\nabla}_{b)}\omega]}\alpha_{c}-(\nabla^{c}\omega)\alpha_{c}\,\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{g_{ab}}\right)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}-1\left(0-(\nabla^{c}\omega)\alpha_{c}\,q_{ab}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\nabla^{c}\omega\right)\alpha_{c}\,q_{ab}.

(7.6)

In the second-to-last row we used that
\omega\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}1
and we made use of the fact that

\delta^{c}{}\underleftarrow{\prescript{}{(a}{\nabla}_{b)}\omega}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}0,

(7.7)

because we take the derivative of
\omega
on
\mathscr{I}^{+}
, where
\omega
is constant. Next, we use the fact that a scalar which is constant on a submanifold, has a gradient which is normal to that submanifold, i.e.,
\left.\nabla^{a}\omega\right|_{\mathscr{I}^{+}}=f\,n^{a}
, where
f
is some smooth function. This delivers the following result.

\displaystyle(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{a}-\mathcal{D}_{a})\underleftarrow{\alpha_{b}}=f\,n^{c}\alpha_{c}\,q_{ab}.

(7.8)

Comparing this equations to equation (
7.2
) finally implies that

\Sigma_{ab}=f\,q_{ab}.

(7.9)

This is a key result and we pause to put it into context: Performing a conformal rescaling with
\omega\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 104.778pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{=}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-104.778pt\raisebox{7.11319pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.64954pt}{\resizebox{8526975}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-104.778pt\hskip 104.778pt}}1
leaves the universal structure invariant but changes the covariant derivative operator. The change in this operator is proportional to the intrinsic metric of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. This proportionality function vanishes if and only if
\omega=1
everywhere, which means it is equivalent to applying the identity transformation to the universal structure. Thus,
f
captures information about the conformal rescaling and represents the “gauge artifact” we described earlier. Furthermore, when the action of two derivative operators differs by
f\,q_{ab}
, we can conclude that the difference is due to the use of two different divergence-free conformal frames. Thus, the difference has nothing to do with physics, but with the arbitrary choice of our conformal frame. This observation leads us to introduce equivalence classes of covariant derivatives. We denote these equivalence classes by
[\mathcal{D}]
and define equivalence as

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}\sim\mathcal{D}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{a}-\mathcal{D}_{b}\right)\alpha_{b}=f\,q_{ab}.

(7.10)

Ultimately, when computing physical quantities using
\mathcal{D}
, they have to be gauge-independent, meaning that they cannot depend on
f
. In yet other words, this means that physical quantities do not depend on our arbitrary choice of a conformal frame.

Our identification of
f
with the rescaling freedom suggests that
\Sigma_{ab}
carries two physical degrees of freedom, by which we mean that
\Sigma_{ab}
carries two degrees of freedom which are independent of the conformal completion we chose and encode information contained in the physical metric
\hat{g}_{ab}
. To see this mathematically, we first isolate the “gauge artifact”
f
by computing the trace of
\Sigma_{ab}
,

q^{ab}\Sigma_{ab}=\frac{1}{2}f.

(7.11)

Observe that the trace is independent of the choice of pseudo-inverse because
\Sigma_{ab}
is transverse to
n^{a}
. The above equation also implies that the trace of
\Sigma_{ab}
is pure gauge and suggests that its trace-free part is the carrier of the physical degrees of freedom. The trace-free part of
\Sigma_{ab}
shall be denoted by
\sigma_{ab}
and it is explicitly given by

\sigma_{ab}:=\Sigma_{ab}-\frac{1}{2}q_{ab}q^{cd}\Sigma_{cd}.

(7.12)

This tensor allows us to distinguish equivalence classes from each other and thus it allows us to distinguish physically distinct spacetimes from each other!

To clarify that, observe that if
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
do
not
differ by
f\,\Sigma_{ab}
they belong to two different equivalence classes and necessarily differ by
\sigma_{ab}
. Thus, it follows that two equivalence classes are disjoint if and only if
\sigma_{ab}\neq 0
,

\displaystyle[\mathcal{D}]\cap[\mathcal{D}]^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\emptyset

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\sigma_{ab}\neq 0.

(7.13)

where
\sigma_{ab}
can be computed with
any
representative of
[\mathcal{D}]
and
[\mathcal{D}]^{\prime}
. Observe that
\sigma_{ab}
is gauge-invariant by construction and that it inherits the following properties from
\Sigma_{ab}
and
q_{ab}
:

\boxed{\begin{aligned} \sigma_{ab}&=\sigma_{(ab)},&&&&&\sigma_{ab}n^{b}&=0,&&&&&q^{ab}\sigma_{ab}&=0\end{aligned}}

(7.14)

In other words,
\sigma_{ab}
is gauge-independent, symmetric, transverse, and trace-free. The last three properties imply that
\sigma_{ab}
carries two independent degrees of freedom. In conjunction with its gauge-independence, this suggests that it carries two degrees of freedom of the physical metric
\hat{g}_{ab}
!

However, below we will give an intuition of how this connection arises before more rigorously deriving the connection in Chapter
8
.

7.B
Radiative Modes and Geometry

In subchapter
6.D
, we have demonstrated that the covariant derivative operator
\mathcal{D}
of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
is completely fixed if we impose one additional condition. Namely, if we know what
\mathcal{D}_{a}\ell_{b}
is, then we completely know
\mathcal{D}
. We have also argued that this equation is what “transfers information” from the physical
\hat{g}_{ab}
to the operator
\mathcal{D}
. In other words, it is this equation which leaves an imprint of the radiative modes in
\mathcal{D}
and, as we have seen in the previous subsection, these radiative modes are potentially encoded in the two degrees of freedom of
\sigma_{ab}
. Hence, there should be a relation between
\mathcal{D}_{a}\ell_{b}
, the tensor
\sigma_{ab}
, and the radiative modes of the gravitational field, which we denote by
h_{+}
and
h_{\times}
. We will show that this is indeed the case, although we will only sketch how
h_{+}
and
h_{\times}
appear in the formalism. A satisfactory derivation will be postponed to Chapter
8
.

Let us begin by expressing the action of
\mathcal{D}
on
\ell_{a}
in terms of
\sigma_{ab}
. To that end, fix a divergence-free conformal frame
(q_{ab},n^{a})
and carry out the construction of a Newman-Penrose null tetrad
(\ell^{a},n^{a},m^{a},\hat{m}^{a})
as described in subsection
3.C
of Chapter
3
. By defining
\mathcal{D}:=\hbox{\lower 3.22916pt\hbox{${}_{\leftharpoondown}$}}\kern-7.3194pt{\nabla}
, we obtain an equivalence class of derivative operators
[\mathcal{D}]
which all satisfy the properties (
6.32
). Then, introduce a
fiducial derivative operator

\mathrel{\vphantom{\square}\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathcal{D}$\cr\raise 2.184pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\tiny{\text{ $\circ$}}$}\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathcal{D}$\cr\raise 2.184pt\hbox{$\textstyle\tiny{\text{ $\circ$}}$}\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}$\cr\raise 1.15903pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tiny{\text{ $\circ$}}$}\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}$\cr\raise 0.46675pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tiny{\text{ $\circ$}}$}\cr}}}}\in[\mathcal{D}]
, defined by the requirement

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vphantom{\square}\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathcal{D}$\cr\raise 2.184pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\tiny{\text{ $\circ$}}$}\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathcal{D}$\cr\raise 2.184pt\hbox{$\textstyle\tiny{\text{ $\circ$}}$}\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}$\cr\raise 1.15903pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tiny{\text{ $\circ$}}$}\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}$\cr\raise 0.46675pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tiny{\text{ $\circ$}}$}\cr}}}}_{a}\ell_{b}\overset{!}{=}0.

The relation between the fiducial derivative and any other derivative
\mathcal{D}\in[\mathcal{D}]
is given by

\displaystyle(\mathrel{\vphantom{\square}\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathcal{D}$\cr\raise 2.184pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\tiny{\text{ $\circ$}}$}\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathcal{D}$\cr\raise 2.184pt\hbox{$\textstyle\tiny{\text{ $\circ$}}$}\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}$\cr\raise 1.15903pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\tiny{\text{ $\circ$}}$}\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}$\cr\raise 0.46675pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\tiny{\text{ $\circ$}}$}\cr}}}}_{a}-\mathcal{D}_{a})\ell_{b}=\Sigma_{ab}n^{c}\ell_{c}=-\Sigma_{ab}=\mathcal{D}_{a}\ell_{b},

where we used
n^{c}\ell_{c}=-1
. Thus, we have found

\mathcal{D}_{a}\ell_{b}=-\Sigma_{ab}.

(7.31)

The trace-free part of
\Sigma_{ab}
can now be expressed in terms of
\mathcal{D}_{a}\ell_{b}
and we introduce a new tensor, known as the
asymptotic shear

\sigma^{\circ}_{ab}
:

\displaystyle\sigma_{ab}^{\circ}:=-\mathcal{D}_{a}\ell_{b}+\frac{1}{2}q_{ab}q^{cd}(\mathcal{D}_{c}\ell_{d}).

(7.32)

This is an important result because it tells us that the gauge-invariant information captured by
\sigma_{ab}
can be computed from
\mathcal{D}_{a}\ell_{b}
. This is precisely what our qualitative picture suggested! Namely that the radiative modes are captured by
\mathcal{D}_{a}\ell_{b}
, because this tensor represents information which is present in
\mathcal{D}
but absent in the universal structure (see discussion in
6.D
). Let us also recall that
\ell_{a}
is
not
part of the universal structure.

To continue our investigation of
\sigma^{\circ}_{ab}
, we choose a
u=u_{0}
cross-section and expand it in a basis of that cross-section. Notice that the properties of the asymptotic shear tell us that on any
u=u_{0}
cross-section it has the form

\displaystyle\sigma_{ab}^{\circ}=-(\bar{\sigma}^{\circ}m_{a}m_{b}+\sigma^{\circ}\bar{m}_{a}\bar{m}_{b}).

(7.33)

The minus sign is convention and the complex function
\sigma^{\circ}
is called the
shear
. Notice that it has spin weight
2
, while its complex conjugate
\bar{\sigma}^{\circ}
has spin weight
-2
.

We now jump ahead and show the relation between the shear and the radiative modes
h_{+},h_{\times}
, but we postpone a proper derivation to Chapter
8
. To that end, notice that it follows from (
7.33
) that the shear is defined as

\sigma^{\circ}(u,\theta,\phi)=-\lim_{r\to\infty}\left(m^{a}m^{b}\nabla_{a}\ell_{b}\right),

(7.34)

where we have extended all fields into the bulk of spacetime. This limit can be explicitly computed and compared with the linearized theory, where
h_{+}
and
h_{\times}
encode the radiative modes. One then finds

\sigma^{\circ}(u,\theta,\phi)=\frac{1}{2}\left(h^{\circ}_{+}+i\,h^{\circ}_{\times}\right)(u,\theta,\phi).

(7.35)

We refer to
h^{\circ}_{+}
and
h^{\circ}_{\times}
as the
strains
of the gravitational wave and define them as

\displaystyle h^{\circ}_{+}

\displaystyle:=\lim_{r\to\infty}rh_{+}

\displaystyle h^{\circ}_{\times}

\displaystyle:=\lim_{r\to\infty}rh_{\times}.

(7.36)

Those unfamiliar with the strain of a gravitational wave, we refer to section
7.C
for a brief introduction. The latter equations explicitly show that there is a relation between
\mathcal{D}_{a}\ell_{b}
and
\sigma_{ab}
, and that
\sigma_{ab}
does indeed carry information about the two radiative degrees of freedom, as we have sketched here.

With this, we conclude our brief outlook on the connection between the strains of the linearized theory and the shear obtained in the non-linear theory. At this point, we wish to develop a deeper understanding of the interplay between geometry and physics. Given a torsion-free and metric-compatible derivative operator, the only other interesting tensor to consider is the curvature tensor
\mathcal{R}_{abc}{}^{d}
. Since we are on the three-dimensional hypersurface
\mathscr{I}^{+}
, we use the letter
\mathcal{R}
for the curvature tensor of
\mathcal{D}
in order to distinguish it from the Riemann tensor
R
defined on
\mathcal{M}
.

With this comment out of the way, we can determine the curvature tensor
\mathcal{R}_{abc}{}^{d}
from

\displaystyle 2\mathcal{D}_{[a}\mathcal{D}_{b]}\alpha_{c}=\mathcal{R}_{abc}{}^{d}\alpha_{d}.

(7.37)

Keeping in mind that in three or less dimensions the Weyl tensor vanishes due to its symmetries, we can decompose
\mathcal{R}_{abc}{}^{d}
solely in terms of the Shouten tensor, which leads to

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{abc}{}^{d}\alpha_{d}=\left(q_{c[a}S_{b]}{}^{d}+S_{c[a}\delta_{b]}{}^{d}\right)\alpha_{d}.

(7.38)

Notice that because
q_{ab}
is degenerate,
S_{ab}=S_{a}{}^{d}q_{bd}
loses some information with respect to
S_{a}{}^{d}
. Also, just as before, we are interested in the “gauge-invariant” part of the curvature, which means we have to study the conformally invariant part of it. To that end, notice that
S_{ab}
as well as
S_{a}{}^{b}
transform under the conformal rescaling
(q_{ab},n^{a})\mapsto(\omega^{2}q_{ab},\omega^{-1}n^{a})
. In fact, one finds

\displaystyle S^{\prime}_{a}{}^{b}

\displaystyle=\omega^{-1}S_{a}{}^{b}-\omega^{-3}\mathcal{D}_{a}(q^{bc}\mathcal{D}_{c}\omega)+4\omega^{-4}(\mathcal{D}_{a}\omega)q^{bc}\mathcal{D}_{c}\omega-\omega^{-4}\delta_{a}{}^{b}(q^{cd}\mathcal{D}_{c}\omega\mathcal{D}_{d}\omega)

\displaystyle S^{\prime}_{ab}

\displaystyle=S_{ab}-2\omega^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{a}\mathcal{D}_{b}\omega+4\omega^{2}(\mathcal{D}_{a}\omega)(\mathcal{D}_{b}\omega)-\omega^{2}q_{ab}(q^{cd}\mathcal{D}_{c}\omega\mathcal{D}_{d}\omega).

(7.39)

In order to obtain the conformally invariant part of
S_{ab}
, it is convenient to work in a Bondi frame. Recall from subsection
6.B
that a Bondi frame is defined by having
q_{ab}
as the metric of the unit
2
-sphere. In this particular frame, one finds that the trace-free part of
S_{ab}
is also its conformally invariant part:

\displaystyle N_{ab}=\underbrace{S_{ab}-\frac{1}{2}q^{cd}S_{cd}q_{ab}}_{\text{conformally invariant}}.

(7.40)

This particular combination of tensors is known in the literature as the
Bondi news tensor
.
25
25

25

Interesting historical side remark: The Bondi news
N
, to be introduced later, describes gravitational radiation in asymptotically flat spacetimes. Since gravitational waves contain information about the processes that created them, one can say that
N
heralds the news that something interesting happened a long time ago and very far away. Back in the 1960’s, a lot of research was geared toward finding this news brought to us by distant phenomena. For more mathematical details, we refer the reader to
[
13
]
One can easily see that the Bondi news inherits the properties
N_{ab}=N_{(ab)}
,
N_{ab}n^{b}=0
and
N_{ab}q^{ab}=0
from the intrinsic metric and the Shouten tensor.

Observe that the curvature tensor is constructed from the derivative operator and that we decomposed it in terms of the Shouten tensor. Thus, the Shouten tensor also depends on the derivative operator. With the Bondi news tensor, we have introduced a new tensor which depends on
\mathcal{D}
in a “gauge-invariant” way. It is therefore natural to ask, what the relation between
N_{ab}
and
\sigma_{ab}
might be. In Exercise
7.4
it is shown that the two tensors are related to each other via

\displaystyle N_{ab}=2\mathcal{L}_{n}\sigma_{ab}^{\circ}=:\dot{\sigma}_{ab}^{\circ}.

(7.41)

This is one of the most important results in these notes! What we derived here is a relationship between a tensor
N_{ab}
, which has been constructed using solely the geometry of the boundary
\mathscr{I}^{+}
, with the shear
\sigma_{ab}
, which encapsulates the radiative degrees of freedom of the bulk. This is a remarkable interplay between physics and geometry.

Because the Bondi news tensor has the properties
N_{ab}=N_{(ab)}
,
N_{ab}n^{b}=0
and
N_{ab}q^{ab}=0
, it is easy to see that it can be written as

\displaystyle N_{ab}=\bar{N}^{\circ}m_{a}m_{b}+N^{\circ}\bar{m}_{a}\bar{m}_{b},

(7.42)

where
N^{\circ}
is a complex function of spin weight
2
which is simply called the
Bondi news
. This expansion closely resembles the one for the asymptotic shear given in (
7.33
). Qualitatively, the Bondi news tensor can also be regarded as the analogue of the Maxwell field strength tensor. Both tensors are conformally invariant and trace-free and, more importantly, the square of
N_{ab}
is proportional to the energy-momentum flux across
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. We will see this in Chapter
8
.

In summary, we found a useful and remarkable interplay between the geometry of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
and the physics of gravitational waves. The conformally invariant part of the curvature of
\mathscr{I}^{+}
is directly related to the strains
h_{+}
,
h_{\times}
, which contain information about gravitational waves produced by distant sources and violet physical phenomena such as the merger of two black holes. Of course, here we have just seen a glimpse of this latter relation between the shear and the strains. We will deepen this connection in Chapter
8
. Moreover, we will revisit physical observables such as the energy carried by gravitational waves and see that the flux through
\mathscr{I}^{+}
can be expressed in terms the Bondi news, or, equivalently, in terms of the strains.

Finally, we will also see how the Newman-Penrose scalars
\Psi^{\circ}_{i}
can be expressed in terms of shear and strains. This is an important result which opens the door to comparing numerical relativity computations with observational data.

Before doing so, however, it is helpful to recall where the two strains
h_{+},h_{\times}
originate from. To that end, we briefly review linearized GR in an interlude.

7.C
Interlude: The Gravitational Wave’s Strain

Let us close this chapter with a brief excursion into the theory of gravitational waves in linearized GR. Here, we want to derive the strains
h_{+},h_{\times}
of a gravitational wave and demonstrate why they play an important role for the detection of such waves. All considerations in this subsection concern the
physical
spacetime only and no conformal transformation is ever needed. We will therefore refrain from using hats on physical quantities.

Let
(\mathcal{M},g_{ab})
be the physical spacetime and let
x^{a}
be coordinates on the manifold. Assume there is a matter distribution described by an energy-momentum tensor
T^{ab}
and assume Einstein’s field equations,

\displaystyle R_{ab}-\frac{1}{2}g_{ab}R=8\pi T_{ab},

(7.43)

hold. Now let us assume that an observer is located far away from a static matter distribution, whose contribution is contained in
T_{ab}
. Hence, it has an effect on spacetime and will be manifested in
g_{ab}
through the field equations. Any change of
T_{ab}
, such as for instance rapid changes in the mass distribution, will induce a change in the gravitational field and thus lead to a change in the metric. For small changes
h_{ab}
, one can write (approximately)

\displaystyle\tilde{g}_{ab}=g_{ab}+h_{ab}.

(7.44)

As we will see later, the perturbations
h_{ab}
can be interpreted as ripples on spacetime, more prominently known as
gravitational waves
.

Assuming that the perturbations are much smaller than unity,
\left|h_{ab}\right|\ll 1
, allows us to linearize the otherwise highly non-linear Einstein field equations. We shall further simplify our computations by assuming that the background metric
g_{ab}
is flat, i.e.,
\tilde{g}_{ab}=\eta_{ab}+h_{ab}
. Note that this technically corresponds to a vacuum background and
T_{ab}=0
. For this scenario, Einstein himself proved that the trace-free part of
h_{ab}
, that is

\displaystyle\tilde{h}_{ab}:=h_{ab}-\frac{1}{2}\eta_{ab}h^{a}{}_{a},

(7.45)

indeed solves a wave equation which admits plane wave solutions similar to the ones of Maxwell’s theory.

At this point, a word about gauge freedom: There are multiple procedures in order to partially or completely fix the gauge freedom that is inherent to GR. Here, we use a very particular gauge choice for the sake of being able to nicely display the form of the gravitational wave solution. In Chapter
8
, on the other hand, we present two alternative gauge fixings, ultimately leading to the same result but taking different routes. The distinction lays in the detail and is of minor relevance for our objective. That said, without loss of generality, we can use the Hilbert gauge
\partial_{a}\tilde{h}^{ab}=0
to show that Einstein’s linearized field equations can be simplified to

\displaystyle\left(-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}+\nabla^{2}\right)\tilde{h}^{ab}\equiv\partial_{a}\partial^{a}\tilde{h}_{ab}=0.

(7.46)

Evidently, this is a wave equation and it does suggest that the perturbations around the background metric are indeed wavelike ripples in spacetime. We remind the reader at this point that under the Hilbert gauge, a transformation
x^{\prime a}=x^{a}+\xi^{a}
induces a change
\tilde{h}^{\prime}_{ab}=\tilde{h}_{ab}-\partial_{a}\xi_{b}-\partial_{b}\xi_{a}+\eta_{ab}\partial_{c}\xi^{c}
and it must hold that
\partial_{a}\partial^{a}\xi^{b}=0
so that
\tilde{h}^{\prime}_{ab}
is again a solution of the above wave equation. The simplest solution to the above wave equation is of the form

\displaystyle\tilde{h}^{ab}=\operatorname{\textsf{Re}}\left[A^{ab}e^{ik_{a}x^{a}}\right],

(7.47)

where
A^{ab}
is a constant, symmetric tensor in which information about amplitude and polarization of the wave is encoded. The wave vector
k_{a}
determines the direction of propagation and the wave’s frequency. Moreover, it is a null vector. Note that the Hilbert gauge implies the constraint
A^{ab}k_{b}=0
, which reduces the number of independent components of
A
to six. Also, note that in physical applications we are solely interested in the real part of
\tilde{h}_{ab}
, hence, we explicitly only considered the real part of (
7.47
).

Using the gauge freedom
\xi^{a}
, we can further reduce the independent components of
A^{ab}
to two by means of a suitable gauge choice, such as the
transverse-traceless gauge
, or
\mathbf{TT}

gauge
for short.
26
26

26

This gauge choice will reappear in Chapter
8
and is subtler than it appears at first sight.
In this gauge, only the spatial components of
\tilde{h}_{ab}
are non-trivial. Note also that transversality here refers to transversality with respect to the direction of propagation, i.e.,
\tilde{h}_{a0}=0
. Moreover, in the
TT
gauge it holds that
\tilde{h}_{ab}=h_{ab}
. Therefore, to avoid confusion, we write
h^{TT}_{ab}
whenever we use the transverse-traceless gauge.

Because
A^{ab}
has precisely two independent components, we can rewrite it in terms of two dimensionless amplitudes
h_{\times},h_{+}
, the
strain
, and real unit polarization tensors
\epsilon^{ab}_{\times},\epsilon^{ab}_{+}
. Explicitly, we can write

\displaystyle A^{ab}=h_{\times}\epsilon^{ab}_{\times}+h_{+}\epsilon^{ab}_{+}.

(7.48)

The exact form of
\epsilon^{ab}_{\times},\epsilon^{ab}_{+}
depends on the direction of propagation and one often sees

\displaystyle h_{ab}^{TT}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0&0\\
0&h_{+}&h_{\times}&0\\
0&h_{\times}&-h_{+}&0\\
0&0&0&0\end{pmatrix}e^{ik_{a}x^{a}}.

(7.49)

Note that
(+)
and
(\times)
are two orthogonal polarization states. That is, it is impossible to construct
(+)
from
(\times)
and vice versa.

Let us try to physically understand what the strains
h_{\times},h_{+}
effectively tell us. In the following, we sketch how the strains influence the motion of test masses. To that end, we consider the linearized Riemann tensor, which reads

\displaystyle R_{abcd}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{a}\partial_{b}h_{cb}+\partial_{c}\partial_{b}h_{ad}-\partial_{a}\partial_{c}h_{bd}-\partial_{b}\partial_{d}h_{ac}\right).

(7.50)

Some components drastically simplify in the
TT
gauge and we find

\displaystyle R^{TT}_{i0j0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}h^{TT}_{ij}

\displaystyle\text{for}i,j\in\{1,2,3\},

(7.51)

which looks similar to Newton’s second law. This gives us a first hint at the role which these particular components of the Riemann tensor play in the
TT
gauge. Indeed, in the Newtonian limit, one can show that

\displaystyle R^{TT}_{i0j0}\approx\frac{\partial^{2}\Phi}{\partial x^{i}\partial x^{j}},

(7.52)

where
\Phi
is Newton’s potential. In this context, the Riemann tensor has an interesting physical interpretation. Namely, it is similar to the tidal force field describing the relative acceleration between two test particles in free fall. Let us clarify this statement: Assuming we have two freely moving test masses in a detector moving on geodesics
x^{a}(\tau)
and
x^{a}(\tau)+\zeta^{a}(\tau)
, respectively, we find a simplified form of the geodesic deviation equation,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}\zeta^{k}}{\mathrm{d}t^{2}}\approx-{R^{k}{}_{0j0}}^{TT}\zeta^{j}.

(7.53)

Hence, we can express the tidal force
f^{k}
as

\displaystyle f^{k}\approx-m\,{R^{k}{}_{0j0}}^{TT}\zeta^{j},

(7.54)

where
m
is the mass of the particle. With this result at hand, let us look at a more interesting example. Consider the case of a detector with two test particles being “hit” by a gravitational wave propagating along the
z
-direction (of our coordinate system). For simplicity, we only consider one polarization and can thus writ

\displaystyle h_{ab}^{TT}=h_{+}\epsilon_{ab}^{TT}\cos[\omega(t-z)],

(7.55)

where
\epsilon^{TT}_{ab}
is an unspecified polarization tensor in the
TT
gauge. Then, as the gravitational wave passes through the detector, the two test masses move relative to each other. This movement can be quantified by the variation
\delta\zeta^{x}
with respect to an initial position
\zeta_{0}^{x}
and one finds

\displaystyle\delta\zeta^{x}=-\frac{1}{2}h_{+}\omega^{2}\cos[\omega(t-z)]\zeta^{x}_{0}

(7.56)

for the variation in
x
-direction and the same expression with a flipped sign for the
y
-direction. This follows from the geodesic deviation equation (
7.53
). The oscillation of the gravitational wave translates directly into a relative motion of the test masses. The force exerted onto the test masses by the passing wave is given by

\displaystyle f^{x}\approx-\frac{m}{2}h_{+}\omega^{2}\cos[\omega(t-z)]\zeta^{x}_{0}

and

\displaystyle f^{y}\approx\frac{m}{2}h_{+}\omega^{2}\cos[\omega(t-z)]\zeta^{y}_{0}.

(7.57)

The latter equations show that the strains
h_{+},h_{\times}
are directly linked to what is measured in gravitational wave detectors, even though we are considering a massively simplified example. They determine the amplitude of the displacement of the test masses. Therefore, linking the strain to the asymptotic shear and subsequently to the Bondi news tensor, with which we can calculate fluxes, resembles a powerful connection between observation and theory. This connection enables us to determine certain quantities based on measurements and do consistency checks with respect to numerical simulations of, for instance, wave form models. More of that will be revealed in the last chapter.

7.D
Exercises

Exercise 7.1

Show that in a conformally completed spacetime
(\mathcal{M},g_{ab})
, the transformation
g_{ab}\mapsto g^{\prime}_{ab}=\omega^{2}g_{ab}
implies that the metric-compatible, torsion-free derivative operator
\nabla
is mapped to
\nabla^{\prime}
with

\displaystyle(\nabla^{\prime}_{a}-\nabla_{a})\alpha_{b}

\displaystyle=C_{ab}{}^{c}\alpha_{c}

with

\displaystyle C_{ab}{}^{c}

\displaystyle=-\omega^{-1}\left(2\delta^{c}{}_{(a}\nabla_{b)}\omega-(\nabla^{c}\omega)g_{ab}\right).

Exercise 7.2

The shear tensor
\sigma_{ab}
is transverse, trace-less, and symmetric. That is, it satisfies
\sigma_{ab}n^{b}=0
,
q^{ab}\sigma_{ab}
, and
\sigma_{[ab]}=0
. Show that this implies that the shear is of the form

\displaystyle\sigma_{ab}=-(\bar{\sigma}^{\circ}m_{a}m_{b}+\sigma^{\circ}\bar{m}_{a}\bar{m}_{b}).

Exercise 7.3

Show that on a 3-dimensional manifold
(\mathscr{I},q_{ab})
with vanishing Weyl contribution, the 3-dimensional Riemann tensor can be decomposed into another rank-2 tensor such that it holds that

\displaystyle R_{abc}{}^{d}\alpha_{d}=\left(q_{c[a}S_{b]}{}^{d}+S_{c[a}\delta_{b]}{}^{d}\right)\alpha_{d}

on this manifold. Further, analyze the properties of
S^{a}{}_{b}
and show that under
(q_{ab},n^{a})\mapsto(\omega^{2}q_{ab},\omega^{-1}n^{a})
we find

\displaystyle S^{\prime}_{a}{}^{b}

\displaystyle=\omega^{-1}S_{a}{}^{b}-\omega^{-3}\mathcal{D}_{a}(q^{bc}\mathcal{D}_{c}\omega)+4\omega^{-4}(\mathcal{D}_{a}\omega)q^{bc}\mathcal{D}_{c}\omega-\omega^{-4}\delta_{a}{}^{b}(q^{cd}\mathcal{D}_{c}\omega\mathcal{D}_{d}\omega)

\displaystyle S^{\prime}_{ab}

\displaystyle=S_{ab}-2\omega^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{a}\mathcal{D}_{b}\omega+4\omega^{2}(\mathcal{D}_{a}\omega)(\mathcal{D}_{b}\omega)-\omega^{2}q_{ab}(q^{cd}\mathcal{D}_{c}\omega\mathcal{D}_{d}\omega)

Using these equations, show that
S_{ca}-\frac{1}{2}q^{mn}S_{mn}q_{ca}
is invariant under the above transformation.

Hint: Recapitulating the statements covered in section
3.B
might be helpful.

Exercise 7.4

Using what we know about the decomposition of the Riemann tensor
R_{abc}{}^{d}=\left(q_{c[a}S_{b]}{}^{d}+S_{c[a}\delta_{b]}{}^{d}\right)
and the asymptotic shear
\sigma_{ab}=-\mathcal{D}_{a}\ell_{b}+\frac{1}{2}q_{ab}q^{cd}(\mathcal{D}_{c}\ell_{d})
, show that

\displaystyle N_{ab}=2\mathcal{L}_{n}\sigma_{ab}^{\circ},

where we defined
N_{ab}
as the Bondi news tensor, which in a Bondi-conformal frame reads
N_{ab}=S_{ab}-\frac{1}{2}q^{cd}S_{cd}q_{ab}
.

Hint: There are multiple ways of approaching this problem. One would be brute force calculation. However, it is useful to work in the Bondi gauge (see
[
14
]
for details and support).

Chapter 8: The Connection between Full and Linearized General Relativity

The aim of this chapter, at least partially, is to draw a connection to what we discussed in the first few chapters. Specifically, our objective is to relate asymptotic shear and the Bondi news to the Newman-Penrose scalars of Chapter
3
. Furthermore, we will also relate all these quantities with the strains
h_{+},h_{\times}
known from the theory of gravitational waves in linearized GR. As we have hinted at in the interlude
7.C
, connecting shear, news, and strains is what opens the door to compare theory with observations.

8.A
Connecting the Newman-Penrose Scalars to Shear and Strain

In order to draw a connection between the Newman-Penrose scalars and the Bondi news tensor, we need to look for an equation which relates the Shouten and the Weyl tensors. Such an equation is given by the Bianchi identities of the Riemann tensor on the conformally completed spacetime. One can show that these equations, in the limit
\Omega\to 0
, reduce to

\displaystyle 2\epsilon^{apq}\mathcal{D}_{[p}S_{q]}^{b}=K^{ab}\equiv\lim_{\Omega\to 0}\left(\Omega^{-1}C^{apbq}n_{p}n_{q}\right).

(8.1)

Recall from Chapter
3
that the Weyl tensor vanishes on
\mathscr{I}^{+}
, but that
\Omega^{-1}C^{abcd}
is
not
zero. In fact,
\Omega^{-1}C^{abcd}
is what we called the
asymptotic Weyl tensor
and we denoted it by
K^{abcd}
. Furthermore, we defined the Newman-Penrose scalars of the conformally completed spacetime with respect to
K^{abcd}
. We repeat these definition here for the convenience of the reader

\displaystyle{\Psi}_{4}

\displaystyle:=K_{abcd}\,n^{a}\bar{m}^{b}n^{c}m^{d}

\displaystyle{\Psi}_{3}

\displaystyle:=K_{abcd}\,\ell^{a}n^{b}\bar{m}^{c}n^{d}

\displaystyle{\Psi}_{2}

\displaystyle:=K_{abcd}\,\ell^{a}m^{b}\bar{m}^{c}n^{d}

\displaystyle{\Psi}_{1}

\displaystyle:=K_{abcd}\,\ell^{a}n^{b}\ell^{c}m^{d}

\displaystyle{\Psi}_{0}

\displaystyle:=K_{abcd}\,\ell^{a}m^{b}\ell^{c}m^{d}.

(8.2)

It follows from these definition, that the tensor
K^{ab}:=\Omega^{-1}C^{apbq}n_{p}n_{q}
can be expressed in terms of
\Psi^{\circ}_{4}
,
\Psi^{\circ}_{3}
, and
\operatorname{\textsf{Im}}\left[\Psi^{\circ}_{2}\right]
. This are five out of ten components of the Weyl tensor. Moreover, because
S_{ab}
is determined by
\mathcal{D}
, which in turn encodes the radiative modes, we can think of
\Psi^{\circ}_{4}
,
\Psi^{\circ}_{3}
, and
\operatorname{\textsf{Im}}\left[\Psi^{\circ}_{2}\right]
as carrying information about gravitational radiation to
\mathscr{I}^{+}
. We also recall the Peeling Theorem from Chapter
4
, which tells us that the
physical

\hat{\Psi}_{4}
decays like
\hat{\Psi}_{4}=\frac{\Psi^{\circ}_{4}}{r}
. This is the expected behavior for radiative modes and, in an analogy with electromagnetism, we call
\Psi^{\circ}_{4}
the
radiation field
.

The relation between the Newman-Penrose scalars and the shear emerges when we contract equation (
8.1
) with the null tetrad components which are needed to construct
\Psi^{\circ}_{4}
,
\Psi^{\circ}_{3}
, and
\Psi^{\circ}_{2}
. For instance, contracting (
8.1
) with
\bar{m}_{a}m_{b}
, and using the symmetries of the Weyl tensor, will make
\Psi_{4}
appear. Contractions with
\ell_{a}\bar{m}_{b}
and
\ell_{a}\bar{m}_{b}
instead, will make
\Psi_{3}
and
\Psi_{2}
appear, respectively. We then use the Leibniz rule in order to make the derivative
\mathcal{D}
act on the null tetrad (sine we know how it has to act on tetrads) and this finally makes the shear appear in our considerations. Explicitly carrying out these steps (see Exercise
8.2
) leads to the three equations

\displaystyle\Psi_{4}^{\circ}

\displaystyle=-\ddot{\bar{\sigma}}^{\circ}

\displaystyle\Psi_{3}^{\circ}

\displaystyle=\eth\dot{\bar{\sigma}}^{\circ}

\displaystyle-2i\operatorname{\textsf{Im}}\left[\Psi_{2}^{\circ}\right]

\displaystyle=\sigma^{\circ}\dot{\bar{\sigma}}^{\circ}-\bar{\sigma}^{\circ}\dot{\sigma}^{\circ}+\eth^{2}\bar{\sigma}^{\circ}-\bar{\eth}^{2}\sigma^{\circ}.

(8.3)

We recall that a dot on the shear symbolizes a derivative with respect to the retarded time coordinate
u
and that
\eth
is the angular derivative operator for spin-weighted function. This operator was introduced in Chapter
2
(cf. equation (
1.58
)). By making use of the relation between the shear and the strain, which we sketched in
7.C
and which we will derive in the next subsection, one can write

\displaystyle\Psi^{\circ}_{4}=-\frac{1}{2}\left(\ddot{h}_{+}^{\circ}-i\ddot{h}_{\times}^{\circ}\right),

(8.4)

where we defined

\displaystyle h^{\circ}_{+}(u,\theta,\phi)

\displaystyle:=\lim_{r\to\infty}rh_{+}(u,r,\theta,\phi),

\displaystyle h^{\circ}_{\times}(u,\theta,\phi)

\displaystyle:=\lim_{r\to\infty}rh_{\times}(u,r,\theta,\phi).

(8.5)

Thus, we have established a connection between the Newman-Penrose scalar
\Psi^{\circ}_{4}
and the strains of the gravitational wave we use in the linearized theory. The label “radiation field” is thus well-justified for
\Psi^{\circ}_{4}
. It is worth remarking that this links theory to observations and data analysis. In fact,
\Psi^{\circ}_{4}
is a key quantity which is computed in Numerical Relativity and integrating it twice over
\mathrm{d}u
